\section{The main component of the pencil configuration space}
\label{sec:main-component}

\Cref{sec:pencil-main-component-route} reduced the hinge-pencil question
to the two main-component statements
(\cref{def:pencil-main-component-statements}): every simple
two-edge-connected multigraph has a pencil realization at the deficiency
rank with adjacent concurrency points distinct, and a generic one
whenever a nondegenerate realization exists at all. This section proves
both over every infinite field (\cref{thm:pencil-x0-generic-attains}),
and with them the pencil conjecture (\cref{thm:pencil-conjecture}). The
rest of this introduction outlines the argument; the subsections follow
it in order.

\emph{The main component.} Draw the bodies in the plane,
$v \mapsto q_v = (x_v, y_v) \in K^2$, with adjacent bodies drawn apart
and no closed neighbourhood drawn on a line, and lift each body to the
point $p_v = (x_v, y_v, z_v, 1) \in K^4$ at a height $z_v$. The lifted
points of every closed neighbourhood lie in a common plane exactly when
the heights agree with an affine function of the drawing on each closed
neighbourhood. That condition is linear, so the heights form a linear
space $L(q)$, the lifting space of the drawing
(\cref{lem:pencil-condition-linear}). Each such configuration is a pencil
realization, with concurrency point $p_v$, with panel the plane through
the closed neighbourhood of $v$, and with the joins $p_u \wedge p_v$ of
adjacent points as hinges (\cref{lem:pencil-config-distinct-realization},
\cref{lem:pencil-config-point-join-rank}). The drawings of least
$\dim L(q)$ contain the non-roots of a nonzero polynomial
(\cref{lem:pencil-x0-main-picture-open}), and over them the
configurations form a vector bundle whose closure is the main component
(\cref{rem:pencil-x0-main-component}). Its general
configuration \emph{attains} when, off the zero set of a nonzero
polynomial in the drawing and, fibre by fibre, off the zero set of a
nonzero polynomial in the heights, the rank is the target
$6(|V(G)| - 1) - \operatorname{def}(\tilde G)$
(\cref{def:pencil-x0-attains}). The rank is lower semicontinuous, so one
configuration at the target over a drawing of least $\dim L(q)$ forces
the general one, and that configuration is itself an adjacent-distinct
realization (\cref{thm:pencil-x0-main-component}). The main component is
never formed: its irreducibility is used only in the form that two
nonempty Zariski-open conditions on one fibre $L(q)$ meet.

\emph{The flat rank and Jackson and Jord\'an's equality.} Write
$\operatorname{def}_2$ and $\operatorname{def}_3$ for the deficiency of
\cref{def:D-deficiency} at $n = 2$ and $n = 3$, so that
$\operatorname{def}_3(G) = \operatorname{def}(\tilde G)$. At a flat
configuration, all heights zero, every hinge lies in one plane and the
rank is exactly $6|V(G)| - 3 - \dim L(q)$ (\cref{thm:pencil-flat-rank});
a partition count bounds $\dim L(q)$ below by
$3 + \operatorname{def}_2(G)$ (\cref{lem:pencil-lifting-space-deficiency}).
At a general drawing of a simple graph the bound is an equality
(\cref{thm:pencil-jj-equality}). This is the form in which the argument
uses Jackson and Jord\'an's pin-collinear theorem~\cite{jacksonJordan2008pin};
here it is derived from the planar case of Katoh and Tanigawa's rank
formula (\cref{thm:theorem-55-6-rows}), over every infinite field. So
where the two deficiencies agree the flat configuration attains
(\cref{cor:pencil-jj-flat}).

\emph{The induction.} The general configuration attains at every graph
satisfying the standing hypotheses, finite, simple and connected with
minimum degree at least two (\cref{def:pencil-x0-standing}), by a strong
induction on the number of bodies (\cref{thm:pencil-x0-reduction-attains},
\cref{thm:pencil-x0-coverage}). Each step takes one drawing that is
general for the smaller graphs and of least $\dim L(q)$ for $G$, and
finds the heights inside the single fibre $L_G(q)$. At a cut vertex and along a
chain of bridges, $G$ is a fibre product of two smaller graphs whose
ranks and deficiencies add (\cref{sec:main-component-cut}). Across an ear
the rank grows with the span of its hinges together with the relative
screws of its two ends. With five or more interior bodies the hinges span
the whole screw space at a general configuration, as one hexagon of joins
certifies (\cref{sec:main-component-chain}). With fewer, the span is
counted at a suitable configuration: with two interior bodies, from three
independent joins; with one, by choosing the drawing of the interior body
together with the heights of the rest; and with three or four, or two at
non-adjacent ends, against $G$ with its second interior body suppressed,
which is then put back on a line through a neighbour
(\cref{sec:main-component-short}, \cref{sec:main-component-orbit}). A body
of degree two whose neighbours are not adjacent is split off as in
Jackson and Jord\'an's proof~\cite{jacksonJordan2008pin}: it starts on the
line through the points of its neighbours and moves along a curve to a
general position, where the rank is at least its starting value
(\cref{sec:main-component-splitoff}). At a rigid core the step is Katoh
and Tanigawa's contraction case~\cite[Lemma~6.3]{katohTanigawa2011}, read
along a curve on which the core shrinks to one point and the heights
solve a rescaled lifting system whose kernel does not jump at the end of
the curve (\cref{sec:main-component-contract},
\cref{sec:main-component-contract-additive}). That every graph
satisfying the standing hypotheses admits one of these steps is a case
analysis on $\operatorname{def}_2$ and $\operatorname{def}_3$ alone
(\cref{sec:main-component-sparse}, \cref{sec:main-component-coverage}): a
bridge or a cut vertex; a cycle; equal deficiencies; a planar-rigid set,
contracted at a maximal one; and otherwise, in a sparse 2-connected graph
that is not a cycle, a chain of bodies of degree two to which an ear or
split-off step applies, or a rigid core at which the planar deficiencies
add (\cref{thm:pencil-x0-theorem-s}). The distinct statement follows
(\cref{lem:pencil-x0-distinct-statement}).

\emph{The generic statement.} It does not follow fibre by fibre: at two
pencil hubs with three common neighbours, $K_{2,3}$ the smallest, no
configuration over a general drawing is nondegenerate
(\cref{lem:pencil-x0-two-hubs-obstruction}). It is proved instead inside
the induction of the pencil reduction (\cref{thm:pencil-reduction}), from
the conditioned pair at every smaller graph
(\cref{thm:pencil-generic-step}). Without a planar-rigid set of two or
more bodies, Jackson and Jord\'an's equality, at $G$ and at $G$ with one
new body joined to two of its bodies, separates the planes of those two
bodies, and the general configuration is nondegenerate
(\cref{thm:pencil-x0-base-generic}). Otherwise a minimal planar-rigid set
gives a body of degree two between two hubs across which the deficiency
does not drop, or a pendant triangle at a body of degree at least four
(\cref{lem:pencil-rigid-good-ear}). The generic realization of the
smaller graph, steered to meet the conditions the new bodies need,
extends across it (\cref{lem:pencil-generic-steer},
\cref{lem:pencil-generic-one-ear},
\cref{lem:pencil-generic-pendant-triangle}). The graphs this induction
meets that are not two-edge-connected are the reduction's cut-edge case
(\cref{lem:pencil-cut-case}), and the result is the conditioned pair at
every multigraph with at least one body
(\cref{thm:pencil-conditioned-pair-nonempty}).

\subsection{Planar pictures, the lifting space, and the main component}
\label{sec:main-component-carrier}

This subsection introduces the configurations of the induction, and
proves the lemma that each step of the induction uses
(\cref{lem:pencil-x0-one-witness}). Throughout, $K$ is an infinite field
and $G$ satisfies the standing hypotheses
(\cref{def:pencil-x0-standing}): it is finite, simple and connected, with
minimum degree at least two. A body of degree $d$ in a simple graph has
$d$ distinct neighbours, so every closed neighbourhood of $G$ has at
least three members.

The idea is to lift a picture of $G$ in the plane, as in scene analysis.
The picture places each body $v$ at a point $q_v = (x_v, y_v)$ of $K^2$,
and a height $z_v$ lifts it to the point $p_v = (x_v, y_v, z_v, 1)$, in
homogeneous coordinates. The picture and the heights form a
configuration. Take the points $p_v$ as concurrency points and the joins
$p_u \wedge p_v$ as hinges. Then the configuration is a pencil
realization when the points of every closed neighbourhood lie in a plane
(\cref{sec:pencil-main-component-route}). We draw the picture with
adjacent bodies at distinct points and with no closed neighbourhood on a
line; such a picture is admissible. The first condition makes every
hinge nonzero. By the second, a plane through the points of a closed
neighbourhood is not vertical, so it is the graph
$\zeta = a\xi + b\eta + c$ of an affine function of $(\xi, \eta)$. The
closed neighbourhood is therefore coplanar exactly when the heights on it
agree with an affine function of the picture
(\cref{lem:pencil-condition-linear}). This condition is linear in the
heights. The heights that satisfy it at every body form the lifting space
$L(q)$. In the language of scene analysis, $L(q)$ is the space of heights
of the liftings of $q$, for the incidence structure whose faces are the
closed neighbourhoods. The heights of one affine function at every body
are the trivial liftings, which put all the faces in one plane (see
Whiteley~\cite{whiteley1996}, \S 8.3).

The rest of the subsection shows that one configuration can stand for
the general one. A configuration over an admissible picture with
$z \in L(q)$ gives a pencil realization
(\cref{lem:pencil-config-distinct-realization}). The general
configuration of $G$ attains when, at a general picture and a general
height in $L(q)$, this realization has the deficiency rank
(\cref{def:pencil-x0-attains}). The main pictures are the admissible
pictures at which $\dim L(q)$ is least. If one configuration over a main
picture has the deficiency rank, then the general configuration attains
(\cref{lem:pencil-x0-one-witness}). We extend its height to heights
$Z(q) \in L(q)$ at general pictures $q$, polynomial in $q$, and use that
the rank is lower semicontinuous. The configurations over the main
pictures form a vector bundle, whose closure is the main component of the
configuration space (\cref{rem:pencil-x0-main-component}). Most steps of
the induction also need one point of a linear space, such as $L(q)$, off
the zero sets of two polynomials that are each nonzero somewhere on it.
Such a point exists, as two nonempty Zariski-open subsets of a linear
space meet (\cref{lem:pencil-x0-fibre-intersection}).

\begin{definition}[Admissible planar picture]
  \label{def:pencil-admissible-picture}
  \lean{Graph.IsAdmissiblePicture,
        CombinatorialRigidity.Molecular.pencilPicturePoint}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-nondegenerate}
  A \emph{planar picture} of $G$ is a map $q \colon V(G) \to K^2$,
  written $q_v = (x_v, y_v)$; its \emph{homogeneous picture points} are
  the vectors $(x_v, y_v, 1) \in K^3$. The picture is \emph{admissible}
  if $q_u \neq q_v$ for every edge $uv$ and, for every body $v$, three
  members of the closed neighbourhood of $v$
  (\cref{def:pencil-nondegenerate}) have linearly independent homogeneous
  picture points, that is, the points $q_w$ of the closed neighbourhood
  do not lie on one line.
\end{definition}

Both conditions are Zariski-open in the coordinates of the picture:
$q_u \neq q_v$ fails only where $x_u - x_v$ and $y_u - y_v$ both vanish,
and three picture points are collinear exactly where the determinant of
their homogeneous picture points vanishes. So the admissible pictures
form a Zariski-open subset of $K^{2|V(G)|}$. It is nonempty. As $K$ is
infinite, we can place the bodies at distinct points $(t, t^2)$ of a
parabola, and no three of these points are collinear.

\begin{definition}[The lifting space]
  \label{def:pencil-lifting-space}
  \lean{Graph.liftingSpace, Graph.affineLifts}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-admissible-picture}
  Let $q$ be a planar picture of $G$. A \emph{height} is a map
  $z \colon V(G) \to K$. The \emph{lifting space} $L(q)$ is the set of
  heights $z$ such that, for every body $v$, the restriction of $z$ to
  the closed neighbourhood of $v$ agrees with an affine function of the
  picture there: there are $a, b, c \in K$ with
  $z_w = a x_w + b y_w + c$ for every $w$ in that neighbourhood. The
  space $\mathrm{Aff}(q)$ of \emph{globally affine} heights is the
  subspace of heights $z_w = \alpha x_w + \beta y_w + \gamma$ for a
  single triple $(\alpha, \beta, \gamma) \in K^3$, the same at every
  body.
\end{definition}

The conditions are linear in $z$, so $L(q)$ is a linear subspace of
$K^{V(G)}$.

\begin{lemma}[The lifting space contains the affine heights]
  \label{lem:pencil-lifting-space-affine}
  \lean{Graph.affineLifts_le_liftingSpace, Graph.finrank_affineLifts,
        Graph.three_le_finrank_liftingSpace}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-lifting-space, def:pencil-admissible-picture}
  Let $q$ be a picture of $G$. Then $\mathrm{Aff}(q) \subseteq L(q)$. If
  $q$ is admissible and $G$ has at least one body, then
  $\mathrm{Aff}(q)$ is three-dimensional, so $\dim L(q) \geq 3$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  A height in $\mathrm{Aff}(q)$ agrees with one affine function of the
  picture at every body, and so on every closed neighbourhood; it lies in
  $L(q)$. The map $(\alpha, \beta, \gamma) \mapsto
  (\alpha x_w + \beta y_w + \gamma)_w$ is linear, with image
  $\mathrm{Aff}(q)$. At an admissible picture it is injective, since an
  affine function that vanishes at three non-collinear picture points of
  a closed neighbourhood is zero. So $\dim \mathrm{Aff}(q) = 3$, and
  $\dim L(q) \geq 3$ by the containment.
\end{proof}

\begin{fmlnote}
  \label{fmlnote:pencil-lifting-space}
  In the formalization a height is a function on the whole ambient set
  of bodies that vanishes off $V(G)$. So $\dim L(q)$ is the dimension of
  a space of heights on $V(G)$, also when $G$ does not contain every
  body.
\end{fmlnote}

The steps of the induction use one picture both for $G$ and for smaller
graphs on some of its bodies, and some steps move the picture at a body
outside a smaller graph. A picture of a larger set of bodies is read as a
picture of $G$ by restriction to $V(G)$.

\begin{lemma}[Admissibility and the lifting space read only the bodies of the graph]
  \label{lem:pencil-picture-local}
  \lean{Graph.isAdmissiblePicture_congr, Graph.liftingSpace_congr}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-admissible-picture, def:pencil-lifting-space}
  Let $q$ and $q'$ be planar pictures of a set of bodies containing
  $V(G)$ that agree at every body of $G$. Then $q$ is admissible for $G$
  if and only if $q'$ is, and $L_G(q) = L_G(q')$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Admissibility reads the picture at the two ends of each edge and on
  each closed neighbourhood, and the lifting space on each closed
  neighbourhood; all of these consist of bodies of $G$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Configurations and their planes]
  \label{def:pencil-configuration}
  \lean{CombinatorialRigidity.Molecular.pencilConfigPoint,
        CombinatorialRigidity.Molecular.pencilNormalOfPicture,
        CombinatorialRigidity.Molecular.pencilNormalOfPicture_ne_zero_iff,
        CombinatorialRigidity.Molecular.pencilNormalOfPicturePoly}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-lifting-space}
  A planar picture $q$ and a height $z$ give the \emph{configuration}
  $p = (q, z)$ of homogeneous points
  $p_v = (x_v, y_v, z_v, 1) \in K^4$. Given a choice of three bodies
  $a_v, b_v, c_v$ at every body $v$, the \emph{plane normal} of $p$ at
  $v$ is the vector $n_v \in K^4$ with
  $n_v \cdot w = \det[p_{a_v}, p_{b_v}, p_{c_v}, w]$ for every
  $w \in K^4$.
\end{definition}

In the pencil realization of a configuration, the panel of a body $v$ is
the plane through the points of its closed neighbourhood. A normal of
that plane is determined only up to a scalar, and we fix one by computing
it from three of the points. The plane normal $n_v$ is orthogonal to
$p_{a_v}$, $p_{b_v}$ and $p_{c_v}$, as a determinant with two equal rows
vanishes. It is nonzero exactly when these three points are linearly
independent, and then $n_v^{\perp}$ is the plane through them. The
coordinates of $n_v$ are polynomials in those of $q$ and $z$.

\begin{lemma}[The plane through three points of a closed neighbourhood contains all of it]
  \label{lem:pencil-selector-plane-contains-nbhd}
  \lean{CombinatorialRigidity.Molecular.dotProduct_pencilNormalOfPicture_eq_zero_of_mem_closedNbhd}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-configuration, def:pencil-lifting-space}
  Let $q$ be a picture, $z \in L(q)$, and $v$ a body of $G$ whose
  three chosen bodies $a_v, b_v, c_v$ lie in the closed neighbourhood
  of $v$. Then the plane normal $n_v$ is orthogonal to $p_w$ for every
  $w$ in the closed neighbourhood of $v$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  As $z \in L(q)$, there are $a, b, c \in K$ with
  $z_w = a x_w + b y_w + c$ for every $w$ in the closed neighbourhood of
  $v$. For each such $w$, the point $p_w$ is therefore the image of the
  homogeneous picture point $(x_w, y_w, 1)$ under the linear map
  $(\xi, \eta, \omega) \mapsto (\xi, \eta, a\xi + b\eta + c\omega, \omega)$
  from $K^3$ to $K^4$. The four points $p_{a_v}, p_{b_v}, p_{c_v}, p_w$
  lie in its image, of dimension at most three. So they are linearly
  dependent, and $n_v \cdot p_w = \det[p_{a_v}, p_{b_v}, p_{c_v}, p_w]$
  vanishes.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The plane normal does not depend on the three bodies, up to a scalar]
  \label{lem:pencil-selector-independent-scalar}
  \lean{CombinatorialRigidity.Molecular.exists_smul_pencilNormalOfPicture_eq_of_mem_closedNbhd}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:pencil-selector-plane-contains-nbhd}
  Let $q$ be admissible, $z \in L(q)$, and $v$ a body of $G$. For two
  choices of three bodies at $v$, each with non-collinear picture
  points and lying in the closed neighbourhood of $v$, the two
  resulting plane normals differ by a nonzero scalar.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The three configuration points of each choice are linearly
  independent, since dropping the height coordinate maps them to
  independent homogeneous picture points. So both plane normals are
  nonzero. The first is orthogonal to the three points of the second
  choice, which lie in the closed neighbourhood
  (\cref{lem:pencil-selector-plane-contains-nbhd}). The second is
  orthogonal to them by definition. The vectors orthogonal to three
  linearly independent vectors of $K^4$ form a line, so the two normals
  are proportional.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[The general configuration attains]
  \label{def:pencil-x0-attains}
  \lean{Graph.X0Attains}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-configuration, def:panel-hinge-framework,
        def:rigidity-matrix, def:D-deficiency}
  The \emph{rank} of a configuration $p = (q, z)$ of $G$ is the rank of
  the rigidity matrix (\cref{def:rigidity-matrix}) of the panel-hinge
  framework on $G$ whose panel normal at each body $v$ is $p_v$
  (\cref{def:panel-hinge-framework}). The \emph{general configuration of
  $G$ attains} if there is a nonzero polynomial $P$ in the picture
  coordinates such that every picture $q$ with $P(q) \neq 0$ is
  admissible and the heights $z \in L(q)$ at which $(q, z)$ has rank
  $6(|V(G)| - 1) - \operatorname{def}(\tilde G)$ (\cref{def:D-deficiency},
  at $n = 3$) contain a nonempty Zariski-open subset of $L(q)$.
\end{definition}

The panel normals in this definition are the points $p_v$, not the plane
normals $n_v$. With the points, the hinge at an edge $uv$ is the meet of
the planes $p_u^{\perp}$ and $p_v^{\perp}$
(\cref{def:panel-support-extensor}). Under the polarity it corresponds
to the line $p_u \wedge p_v$, the hinge of the pencil realization, and it
is nonzero whenever $q_u \neq q_v$
(\cref{lem:pencil-config-point-join-rank}). With the plane normals, the
hinge would be zero wherever two adjacent planes coincide, and a zero
hinge allows its two bodies no relative motion.

Over an infinite field a nonzero polynomial has a non-root, so the
pictures $q$ with $P(q) \neq 0$ form a nonempty Zariski-open set. Over a
finite field a nonzero polynomial can vanish at every point, and the
definition then holds vacuously.

\begin{lemma}[The rank of a configuration is the rank of the joins of its points]
  \label{lem:pencil-config-point-join-rank}
  \lean{CombinatorialRigidity.Molecular.finrank_span_rigidityRows_pencilConfigFramework,
        CombinatorialRigidity.Molecular.pencilConfigFramework,
        Graph.IsAdmissiblePicture.pencilConfigFramework_supportExtensor_ne_zero}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-x0-attains, def:body-hinge-framework, def:extensor,
        lem:panel-hinge-dual-molecular, lem:screw-map-rows}
  Let $p = (q, z)$ be a configuration of $G$, and let $F_p$ be the
  body-hinge framework on $G$ (\cref{def:body-hinge-framework}) whose
  hinge at each edge $uv$ is the line $p_u \wedge p_v$
  (\cref{def:extensor}). Then the rigidity matrix of $F_p$ has the rank
  of $p$ (\cref{def:pencil-x0-attains}). If $q$ is admissible, every
  hinge of $F_p$ is nonzero.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The hinge at $uv$ of the panel-hinge framework that defines the rank
  of $p$ is the meet of the planes $p_u^{\perp}$ and $p_v^{\perp}$. It is
  the image of $p_u \wedge p_v$ under the polarity
  (\cref{lem:panel-hinge-dual-molecular}), an invertible linear map of
  the screw space over every field. So the two rigidity matrices have the
  same rank (\cref{lem:screw-map-rows}). Over an admissible picture,
  adjacent picture points differ. A relation
  $\lambda\, p_u + \mu\, p_v = 0$ then has $\lambda + \mu = 0$ in the last
  coordinate and $\lambda\,(q_u - q_v) = 0$ in the first two, so
  $\lambda = \mu = 0$. Hence $p_u$ and $p_v$ are linearly independent,
  and their join is nonzero.
\end{proof}

\begin{fmlnote}
  \label{fmlnote:pencil-config-point-join-rank}
  A body-hinge framework has a hinge at every label of the ambient set
  of edge labels, also at labels that are not edges of $G$. There the
  formal framework $F_p$ has one fixed nonzero line. No rigidity row
  reads it, and it keeps every hinge nonzero, as a pencil realization
  requires.
\end{fmlnote}

\begin{lemma}[A configuration over an admissible picture is a pencil realization]
  \label{lem:pencil-config-distinct-realization}
  \lean{Graph.IsAdmissiblePicture.hasDistinctPencilRealization,
        Graph.IsAdmissiblePicture.hasPencilPanelRealization_pencilConfigFramework}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:pencil-config-point-join-rank,
        lem:pencil-selector-plane-contains-nbhd,
        def:pencil-panel-realization, def:pencil-distinct-motive}
  Let $q$ be an admissible picture of $G$, $z \in L(q)$ and
  $p = (q, z)$. Choose at every body $v$ three members of its closed
  neighbourhood with non-collinear picture points, and let $n_v$ be the
  resulting plane normal (\cref{def:pencil-configuration}). Then the
  framework $F_p$ of \cref{lem:pencil-config-point-join-rank}, with
  panel normals $n_v$ and concurrency points $p_v$, is a pencil
  realization of $G$ (\cref{def:pencil-panel-realization}) whose
  adjacent points are linearly independent. If moreover $p$ has rank
  $6(|V(G)| - 1) - \operatorname{def}(\tilde G)$, then $G$ has an
  adjacent-distinct pencil realization (\cref{def:pencil-distinct-motive},
  at $n = 3$).
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The three chosen configuration points at $v$ are linearly independent,
  since their picture points are, so $n_v \neq 0$. And $n_v$ is
  orthogonal to $p_w$ for every $w$ in the closed neighbourhood of $v$
  (\cref{lem:pencil-selector-plane-contains-nbhd}).

  The two ends of an edge $uv$ lie in the closed neighbourhoods of both
  $u$ and $v$. So $p_u$ and $p_v$ are orthogonal to $n_u$ and to $n_v$:
  the line $p_u \wedge p_v$ lies in both panels, and it passes through
  both points by construction. Taking $w = v$ gives the incidence
  $p_v \cdot n_v = 0$, and $p_v \neq 0$ since its last coordinate is $1$.
  Adjacent points are linearly independent, and every hinge is nonzero
  (\cref{lem:pencil-config-point-join-rank}). By the same lemma the rank
  of $F_p$ is the rank of $p$.
\end{proof}

The next lemma proves the equivalence on which the lifting space rests.
It also gives the plane of each body explicitly, in the form that
\cref{lem:pencil-x0-conjunct-three} uses.

\begin{lemma}[Coplanarity is linear in the heights]
  \label{lem:pencil-condition-linear}
  \lean{Graph.IsAdmissiblePicture.mem_liftingSpace_of_coplanar,
        Graph.IsAdmissiblePicture.exists_smul_eq_interpolant}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-lifting-space, def:pencil-configuration,
        def:pencil-panel-realization}
  Let $q$ be admissible, $z$ a height and $p = (q, z)$. The points of
  every closed neighbourhood of $p$ lie in a plane if and only if
  $z \in L(q)$. In that case $p$ gives a pencil realization
  (\cref{def:pencil-panel-realization}) whose adjacent points are
  distinct, and the plane through the points of the closed neighbourhood
  of each body $v$ is unique and not vertical: if
  $z_w = a x_w + b y_w + c$ on that neighbourhood, every nonzero vector
  orthogonal to its points is a multiple of $(a, b, -1, c)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:pencil-selector-plane-contains-nbhd,
        lem:pencil-selector-independent-scalar,
        lem:pencil-config-distinct-realization}
  Suppose that $z \in L(q)$. Admissibility gives at each body $v$ three
  members of its closed neighbourhood with non-collinear picture points.
  Their configuration points are linearly independent, as their picture
  points are, so their plane normal is nonzero. It is orthogonal to the
  points of the whole closed neighbourhood
  (\cref{lem:pencil-selector-plane-contains-nbhd}). So these points lie
  in a plane.

  Conversely, suppose that the points of every closed neighbourhood lie
  in a plane. At a body $v$, let $n \neq 0$ be orthogonal to them, so
  that $n_0 x_w + n_1 y_w + n_2 z_w + n_3 = 0$ for every $w$ in the
  closed neighbourhood of $v$. If $n_2$ were zero, $(n_0, n_1, n_3)$
  would be orthogonal to the homogeneous picture points of the three
  members that admissibility gives, which are linearly independent, and
  $n$ would vanish. So $n_2 \neq 0$, and solving for $z_w$ shows that $z$
  agrees with an affine function of the picture on the closed
  neighbourhood. Hence $z \in L(q)$.

  The pencil realization, with adjacent points distinct, is that of
  \cref{lem:pencil-config-distinct-realization}. If
  $z_w = a x_w + b y_w + c$ on the closed neighbourhood of $v$, the
  vector $(a, b, -1, c)$ is orthogonal to its points. The points of the
  three members above are linearly independent, so the vectors orthogonal
  to them form a line, as in
  \cref{lem:pencil-selector-independent-scalar}. Hence every nonzero
  vector orthogonal to the closed neighbourhood is a multiple of
  $(a, b, -1, c)$, and its height coordinate is nonzero: the plane is not
  vertical.
\end{proof}

The next lemma is stated for every invertible linear map of $K^4$,
because we need two such maps. One scales and shifts the heights
(\cref{lem:pencil-rank-scale-shift}). The other is the homothety that
shrinks a core along the contraction curve
(\cref{lem:pencil-contract-magnified-rank}).

\begin{lemma}[A collineation of the normals preserves the rank]
  \label{lem:pencil-rank-collineation}
  \lean{CombinatorialRigidity.Molecular.PanelHingeFramework.finrank_span_rigidityRows_ofNormals_linearEquiv}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:panel-hinge-framework, def:rigidity-matrix}
  Let $G$ be a graph, and let $g$ be an invertible linear map of $K^4$.
  If panel normals $p$ and $p'$ in $K^4$ satisfy $p'_w = g(p_w)$ at every
  body $w$ of $G$, then the rigidity matrices of the panel-hinge
  frameworks on $G$ with normals $p$ and with normals $p'$ have the same
  rank.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:panel-hinge-dual-molecular, lem:screw-map-rows,
        lem:pencil-config-point-join-rank}
  The hinge at an edge $uv$ with normals $p$ is the image of the line
  $p_u \wedge p_v$ under the polarity
  (\cref{lem:panel-hinge-dual-molecular}). So the rigidity matrix with
  normals $p$ has the rank of the body-hinge framework whose hinge at
  $uv$ is $p_u \wedge p_v$, as in the proof of
  \cref{lem:pencil-config-point-join-rank}, and likewise for $p'$. Both
  ends of an edge of $G$ are bodies of $G$. So
  $p'_u \wedge p'_v = g(p_u) \wedge g(p_v)$ is the image of
  $p_u \wedge p_v$ under the invertible linear map of the screw space
  induced by $g$, and the two ranks agree (\cref{lem:screw-map-rows}).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Scaling and affine shifts of the heights preserve the rank]
  \label{lem:pencil-rank-scale-shift}
  \lean{Graph.finrank_span_rigidityRows_ofNormals_smul_add_affineLifts}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:pencil-config-point-join-rank, def:pencil-lifting-space,
        lem:screw-map-rows}
  Let $q$ be a picture of $G$, $z$ a height, $t \in K$ nonzero and
  $a \in \mathrm{Aff}(q)$. Then the configurations $(q, z)$ and
  $(q, tz + a)$ have the same rank (\cref{def:pencil-x0-attains}).
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:pencil-rank-collineation}
  Write $a_w = \alpha x_w + \beta y_w + \gamma$. The linear map
  $(\xi, \eta, \zeta, \omega) \mapsto
  (\xi, \eta, t\zeta + \alpha\xi + \beta\eta + \gamma\omega, \omega)$ of
  $K^4$ is invertible, since $t \neq 0$. At every body $w$ it maps the
  point $(x_w, y_w, z_w, 1)$ of $(q, z)$ to the point
  $(x_w, y_w, t z_w + a_w, 1)$ of $(q, tz + a)$. So
  \cref{lem:pencil-rank-collineation} applies.
\end{proof}

The proof of \cref{lem:pencil-x0-one-witness} extends a height in
$L(q_0)$, for a picture $q_0$, to heights at a general picture. For this
it needs $\dim L(q) \ge \dim L(q_0)$ at a general picture $q$, which
holds when $\dim L(q_0)$ is least.

\begin{definition}[Main pictures]
  \label{def:pencil-main-picture}
  \lean{Graph.IsMainPicture}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-lifting-space}
  Let $\ell_0$ be the least value of $\dim L(q)$ over admissible
  pictures $q$ of $G$. A \emph{main picture} of $G$ is an admissible
  picture $q$ with $\dim L(q) = \ell_0$, and $U$ denotes the set of main
  pictures.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}[The geometry of the main component]
  \label{rem:pencil-x0-main-component}
  Over the set $U$ of main pictures, $\dim L(q)$ is constant, equal to
  $\ell_0$. Moreover $L(q)$ is the projection to the heights of the
  solutions of a linear system whose coefficients are polynomials in $q$
  (the proof of \cref{lem:pencil-x0-one-witness}). So the pairs $(q, z)$
  with $q \in U$ and $z \in L(q)$ form a vector bundle over $U$. Its
  closure $X_0$ is the \emph{main component} of the pencil configuration
  space. It is irreducible of dimension $2|V(G)| + \ell_0$, and it
  contains every flat configuration $(q, 0)$ with $q \in U$. We never
  form $X_0$ itself. In our statements, ``general'' means off the zero
  set of a nonzero polynomial in the picture coordinates and, over each
  picture, off the zero set of a nonzero polynomial in the heights
  (\cref{def:pencil-x0-attains}). In place of the irreducibility of
  $X_0$ we use that of the space of pictures and of each fibre $L(q)$
  (\cref{lem:pencil-x0-fibre-intersection}).
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}[One attaining configuration forces the general one]
  \label{lem:pencil-x0-one-witness}
  \lean{Graph.x0Attains_of_exists,
        Graph.IsAdmissiblePicture.exists_mvPolynomial,
        Graph.IsAdmissiblePicture.supportExtensor_ne_zero,
        Graph.liftingMatrix, Graph.map_ker_liftingMatrix,
        Graph.finrank_ker_liftingMatrix,
        Matrix.exists_mvPolynomial_section_mulVec_eq_zero}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-main-picture, def:pencil-x0-attains,
        def:panel-support-extensor, lem:rank-polynomial-of-le-finrank,
        lem:relative-deficiency-rank-bound}
  Let $G$ have at least one body, let $q_0$ be a main picture of $G$
  (\cref{def:pencil-main-picture}) and $z_0 \in L(q_0)$, and suppose the
  configuration $(q_0, z_0)$ has rank at least
  $6(|V(G)| - 1) - \operatorname{def}(\tilde G)$
  (\cref{def:pencil-x0-attains}). Then the general configuration of $G$
  attains.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \emph{The lifting system.} Let $M(q)$ be the homogeneous linear system
  in the heights $z$ and one coefficient vector $h_v \in K^3$ per body
  $v$ that requires $z_w = h_v \cdot (x_w, y_w, 1)$ for every $w$ in the
  closed neighbourhood of $v$. Its coefficients are polynomials in $q$,
  and the heights of its solutions form $L(q)$. Over an admissible
  picture the heights of a solution determine each $h_v$, as three
  members of the closed neighbourhood of $v$ have non-collinear picture
  points. So there
  the solutions form a space of dimension $\dim L(q)$. As $q_0$ is a main
  picture, this dimension is at least $\dim L(q_0)$ at every admissible
  picture $q$.

  \emph{A polynomial section.} Near a point, the kernel of a matrix of
  polynomials can only lose dimension. Where it keeps its dimension, a
  kernel vector at the point extends to kernel vectors nearby that are
  polynomial in the parameters, and Cramer's rule writes them down. We
  state this in general, since the contraction steps use it for the
  rescaled lifting system, with the one parameter $t$
  (\cref{def:pencil-contract-lifting-system}). Let $M(s)$ be a matrix
  whose entries are polynomials in parameters $s$, and let
  $M(s_0)\, x_0 = 0$. Then there are a polynomial $D$ with
  $D(s_0) \neq 0$ and a vector $X(s)$ of polynomials with
  $X(s_0) = x_0$, such that $\dim \ker M(s) \le \dim \ker M(s_0)$
  wherever $D(s) \neq 0$, and $M(s)\, X(s) = 0$ wherever moreover
  $\dim \ker M(s) \ge \dim \ker M(s_0)$.

  To prove it, let $\pi$ be a projection onto $\ker M(s_0)$. The stacked
  matrix $[M(s_0); \pi]$, with the rows of $M(s_0)$ above those of $\pi$,
  is injective, as $\pi$ fixes every vector of $\ker M(s_0)$. Let $H$ be
  a left inverse of it, and let $S(s) = H\,[M(s); \pi]$. This is a square
  matrix of polynomials with $S(s_0) = 1$. Put $D = \det S$. Where
  $D(s) \neq 0$ the stack $[M(s); \pi]$ is injective, so $\pi$ maps
  $\ker M(s)$ injectively into $\ker M(s_0)$. If moreover
  $\dim \ker M(s) \ge \dim \ker M(s_0)$, this map is onto, and some
  $x \in \ker M(s)$ has $\pi x = x_0$. Then
  $S(s)\, x = H\,[0; x_0] = S(s_0)\, x_0 = x_0$. By Cramer's rule
  $X(s) = \operatorname{adj} S(s)\, x_0$ equals $D(s)\, x$, so it lies in
  $\ker M(s)$. And $X(s_0) = x_0$, as $S(s_0) = 1$.

  \emph{The rank.} The rank of a configuration is the rank of the
  panel-hinge framework whose panel normals are its points. Over an
  admissible picture its hinges are nonzero
  (\cref{def:panel-support-extensor}), since two points $(x, y, z, 1)$
  over distinct picture points are linearly independent. So
  \cref{lem:rank-polynomial-of-le-finrank} gives a polynomial $Q$ in the
  coordinates of the points, nonzero at $(q_0, z_0)$, such that the rank
  is at least $6(|V(G)| - 1) - \operatorname{def}(\tilde G)$ wherever $Q$
  is nonzero. Wherever every hinge is nonzero, the rank is at most this
  value (\cref{lem:relative-deficiency-rank-bound}).

  \emph{The polynomials $P$ and $R$.} Some polynomial $A$ in the picture
  coordinates, nonzero at $q_0$, has only admissible non-roots. For
  instance, take the product of one coordinate difference for each pair
  of adjacent bodies, in a coordinate in which they differ at $q_0$, and,
  for each body, the determinant of the homogeneous picture points of
  three members of its closed neighbourhood that are independent at
  $q_0$. Lift $z_0$ to a solution $x_0$ of $M(q_0)$, and let $D$ and $X$
  be as above, with $Z(q)$ the heights of $X(q)$. Let $P$ be the product
  of $A$, $D$ and $Q$ at the configuration $(q, Z(q))$. Then
  $P(q_0) \neq 0$. A picture $q$ with $P(q) \neq 0$ is admissible, and
  $Z(q) \in L(q)$. For such $q$, let $R$ be $Q$ at $(q, \cdot)$, a
  polynomial in the heights with $R(Z(q)) \neq 0$. At every
  $z \in L(q)$ with $R(z) \neq 0$ the two bounds above give $(q, z)$ the
  rank $6(|V(G)| - 1) - \operatorname{def}(\tilde G)$.
\end{proof}

Later steps use the solutions $(z, h)$ of the lifting system $M(q)$ of
this proof. The contraction steps use the same system with other vectors
in place of the homogeneous picture points $(x_w, y_w, 1)$
(\cref{def:pencil-contract-lifting-system}).

\begin{definition}[The lifting system with weights]
  \label{def:pencil-weighted-lifting-system}
  \lean{Graph.weightedLiftingMatrix}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-lifting-space}
  Let $\omega$ assign a vector $\omega(v, w) \in K^3$ to every ordered
  pair of bodies. The \emph{lifting system with weights $\omega$} of $G$
  is the homogeneous linear system in the heights $z$ and one coefficient
  vector $h_v \in K^3$ per body $v$ of $G$ requiring
  $z_w = h_v \cdot \omega(v, w)$ for every body $v$ and every $w$ in the
  closed neighbourhood of $v$.
\end{definition}

With $\omega(v, w) = (x_w, y_w, 1)$ this is the lifting system $M(q)$
of $G$ at $q$, and the heights of its solutions form $L(q)$.

\begin{lemma}[The main pictures are nonempty and Zariski-open]
  \label{lem:pencil-x0-main-picture-open}
  \lean{Graph.exists_isAdmissiblePicture, Graph.exists_isMainPicture,
        Graph.exists_mvPolynomial_isMainPicture}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-main-picture}
  There is a nonzero polynomial $P$ in the picture coordinates such that
  every picture $q$ with $P(q) \neq 0$ is a main picture. In particular
  the set $U$ of main pictures is nonempty and Zariski-open.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:pencil-x0-one-witness}
  Admissible pictures exist: place the bodies at distinct points of the
  parabola $\{(t, t^2)\}$, as after \cref{def:pencil-admissible-picture}.
  So $\ell_0$ exists, and it is attained at a main picture $q_0$. Apply the
  polynomial section of the proof of \cref{lem:pencil-x0-one-witness} to
  $M(q)$ at $q_0$ and the zero solution. It gives a polynomial $D$,
  nonzero at $q_0$, such that wherever $D(q) \neq 0$ the solutions of
  $M(q)$ form a space of dimension at most $\ell_0$. Let $P$ be the
  product of $D$ and a polynomial nonzero at $q_0$ whose non-roots are
  admissible, as in that proof. At a non-root $q$ of $P$, $q$ is
  admissible and $\dim L(q) \le \ell_0$, so $q$ is a main picture.
\end{proof}
\begin{fmlnote}
  \label{fmlnote:pencil-x0-main-picture-open}
  In place of the standing hypotheses on $G$, the formal statements
  assume the two consequences the construction uses: $G$ has no loops,
  and every closed neighbourhood has at least three members.
\end{fmlnote}

\begin{lemma}[The general configuration attaining gives an adjacent-distinct realization]
  \label{lem:pencil-x0-attains-distinct}
  \lean{Graph.X0Attains.hasDistinctPencilRealization}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-x0-attains, lem:pencil-config-distinct-realization,
        def:pencil-distinct-motive}
  Let $K$ be infinite. If the general configuration of $G$ attains
  (\cref{def:pencil-x0-attains}), then $G$ has an adjacent-distinct
  pencil realization (\cref{def:pencil-distinct-motive}, at $n = 3$).
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  As $K$ is infinite, the nonzero polynomial $P$ of
  \cref{def:pencil-x0-attains} has a non-root $q$. This picture is
  admissible, and some $z \in L(q)$ gives $(q, z)$ the rank
  $6(|V(G)| - 1) - \operatorname{def}(\tilde G)$. Apply
  \cref{lem:pencil-config-distinct-realization} to $(q, z)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Two open conditions on a fibre meet]
  \label{lem:pencil-x0-fibre-intersection}
  \lean{MvPolynomial.exists_mem_eval_ne_zero₂}
  \leanok
  Let $K$ be infinite and let $L$ be a linear subspace of $K^\sigma$
  for an index set $\sigma$. If two polynomials in the coordinates of
  $\sigma$ are each nonzero somewhere on $L$, some single point of $L$
  makes both nonzero.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Let $z_1, z_2 \in L$ be points at which the first and the second
  polynomial are nonzero. On the line $z_1 + t\,(z_2 - z_1)$, $t \in K$,
  each polynomial restricts to a polynomial in $t$. The first restriction
  is nonzero at $t = 0$ and the second at $t = 1$, so their product is a
  nonzero polynomial in one variable. As $K$ is infinite, it has a
  non-root $t$. The point $z_1 + t\,(z_2 - z_1)$ lies in $L$, and both
  polynomials are nonzero there.
\end{proof}

The next theorem states the main results of this subsection together.
Later subsections cite the lemmas themselves.

\begin{theorem}[The main component]
  \label{thm:pencil-x0-main-component}
  \lean{Graph.exists_mvPolynomial_isMainPicture,
        MvPolynomial.exists_mem_eval_ne_zero₂,
        Graph.x0Attains_of_exists,
        Graph.X0Attains.hasDistinctPencilRealization}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-main-picture, def:pencil-x0-attains,
        def:pencil-distinct-motive, lem:pencil-x0-main-picture-open,
        lem:pencil-x0-fibre-intersection, lem:pencil-x0-one-witness,
        lem:pencil-x0-attains-distinct}
  Let $K$ be infinite, and let $G$ have at least one body, no loops, and
  at least three members in every closed neighbourhood. Then the main
  pictures of $G$ (\cref{def:pencil-main-picture}) contain the non-roots
  of a nonzero polynomial in the picture coordinates. Over any picture
  $q$, two polynomials in the heights that are each nonzero somewhere on
  $L(q)$ are nonzero together somewhere on $L(q)$. Moreover, if a single
  configuration $(q, z)$ with $q$ a main picture and $z \in L(q)$ has
  rank at least $6(|V(G)| - 1) - \operatorname{def}(\tilde G)$, then the
  general configuration of $G$ attains (\cref{def:pencil-x0-attains}),
  and $G$ has an adjacent-distinct pencil realization
  (\cref{def:pencil-distinct-motive}, at $n = 3$).
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The first claim is \cref{lem:pencil-x0-main-picture-open}. The second
  is \cref{lem:pencil-x0-fibre-intersection}, applied to the linear
  subspace $L(q)$ of the heights. For the third, the general
  configuration attains by \cref{lem:pencil-x0-one-witness}, and then
  $G$ has an adjacent-distinct pencil realization
  (\cref{lem:pencil-x0-attains-distinct}).
\end{proof}

\subsection{The flat rank}
\label{sec:main-component-flat}

This subsection computes the rank of a \emph{flat} configuration
$(q, 0)$, with all heights zero, over an admissible picture $q$. The rank
is exactly $6|V(G)| - 3 - \dim L(q)$ (\cref{thm:pencil-flat-rank}). With
Jackson and Jord\'an's equality, the next subsection derives from it
that, under the standing hypotheses, the general configuration attains
whenever the two deficiencies agree (\cref{cor:pencil-jj-flat}). This
settles one case of the induction with no appeal to smaller graphs
(\cref{sec:main-component-coverage}).
Throughout, $\operatorname{def}_2$ and $\operatorname{def}_3$ are the
deficiencies of \cref{def:D-deficiency} at $n = 2$ and $n = 3$ ($D = 3$
and $D = 6$).

The idea is that at zero heights every hinge $p_u \wedge p_v$ is a line
of the plane $z = 0$. We split the screw space into the screws of the
lines of this plane and a complement (\cref{lem:pencil-flat-split}). At a
hinge, the screws of the two bodies may differ only by a multiple of the
hinge. So the hinges glue the complementary parts of the bodies' screws
together, and on a connected graph these parts are all equal. They give
three dimensions of motions. The screws of the lines of the plane
correspond to the affine functions on $K^2$, a line to the functions
vanishing on it. So the rest of a motion is a family $(h_v)$ of affine
functions such that $h_u - h_v$ vanishes at $q_u$ and at $q_v$ for every
edge $uv$. These families are the lifting planes
(\cref{def:pencil-lifting-planes}). Evaluating each $h_v$ at $q_v$
identifies them with the lifting space $L(q)$
(\cref{lem:pencil-lifting-planes-dim}), and rank--nullity gives the flat
rank.

Over the reals, $h_v$ gives the velocities normal to the plane $z = 0$ of
the points of that plane moving with the body $v$. The planes
$\zeta = h_v(\xi, \eta)$ are then the faces of a polyhedron over the
picture, a lifting as in \cref{sec:main-component-carrier}. Crapo and
Whiteley~\cite{crapoWhiteley1982} describe this correspondence between
the motions of a flat panel structure and the polyhedra over it for the
tetrahedron, and note that it holds in great generality (Example~4.4).
We prove what we need directly.

The lifting planes also form the motion space of a panel-hinge framework
$P_q$ in the plane, with panel normal $(x_v, y_v, 1)$ at each body $v$
(\cref{lem:pencil-lifting-planes-motions}). A partition count on $P_q$
bounds $\dim L(q)$ below by $3 + \operatorname{def}_2(G)$
(\cref{lem:pencil-lifting-space-deficiency}). So the flat rank is at most
$6(|V(G)| - 1) - \operatorname{def}_2(G)$, with equality where the bound
on $\dim L(q)$ is attained. Jackson and Jord\'an's equality says that,
under the standing hypotheses, it is attained at a general picture
(\cref{thm:pencil-jj-equality}). Since $\operatorname{def}_3(G) \le
\operatorname{def}_2(G)$ (\cref{lem:deficiency-antitone}), the flat
configuration has the deficiency rank
$6(|V(G)| - 1) - \operatorname{def}_3(G)$ only if the two deficiencies
agree. When it has, it is a witness for \cref{lem:pencil-x0-one-witness}
(\cref{cor:pencil-flat-attains}). The last corollary is the case
$\dim L(q) = 3$, which the contraction step uses at a core of planar
deficiency zero (\cref{sec:main-component-contract}).

\begin{definition}[The lifting planes]
  \label{def:pencil-lifting-planes}
  \lean{Graph.liftingPlanes}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-admissible-picture}
  Let $q$ be a planar picture of $G$, and identify a vector
  $h \in K^3$ with the affine function $(x, y) \mapsto h \cdot (x, y, 1)$
  on $K^2$. The space $F(q)$ of \emph{lifting planes} is the set of
  families $(h_v)_{v \in V(G)}$ of affine functions with
  $(h_u - h_v)(q_u) = (h_u - h_v)(q_v) = 0$ for every edge $uv$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[The lifting planes and the lifting space]
  \label{lem:pencil-lifting-planes-dim}
  \lean{Graph.finrank_liftingPlanes}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-lifting-planes, def:pencil-lifting-space}
  Let $q$ be admissible. Then $\dim F(q) = \dim L(q)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Define $\Phi(h)_w = h_w(q_w)$. Let $v$ be a body. For a neighbour $u$
  of $v$, $h_u(q_u) = h_v(q_u)$, as $h_u - h_v$ vanishes at $q_u$. So on
  the closed neighbourhood of $v$, $\Phi(h)$ agrees with the affine
  function $h_v$, and $\Phi(h) \in L(q)$.

  Conversely, let $z \in L(q)$. For each body $v$, let $h_v$ be an affine
  function that agrees with $z$ on the closed neighbourhood of $v$. Both
  ends of an edge $uv$ lie in both closed neighbourhoods, so $h_u - h_v$
  vanishes at $q_u$ and $q_v$. Hence $(h_v)_v \in F(q)$, and $\Phi$ maps
  it to $z$.

  If $\Phi(h) = 0$, then $h_v$ vanishes at the picture point of every
  member of the closed neighbourhood of $v$. Three of these points are
  not collinear, as $q$ is admissible, so $h_v = 0$. Hence $\Phi$ is an
  isomorphism.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The motions of a flat configuration]
  \label{lem:pencil-flat-split}
  \lean{CombinatorialRigidity.Molecular.flatScrewEquiv,
        Graph.finrank_linkConstants,
        Graph.finrank_infinitesimalMotions_pointJoinFramework_flat}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-lifting-planes, lem:pencil-config-point-join-rank}
  Let $G$ be connected, let $q$ be admissible, and let $F_{(q,0)}$ be the
  body-hinge framework whose hinge at each edge $uv$ is the line
  $p_u \wedge p_v$ of the flat configuration $p = (q, 0)$
  (\cref{lem:pencil-config-point-join-rank}). Then its infinitesimal
  motions form a space of dimension $3 + \dim F(q)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:extensor, def:hinge-constraint}
  Let $e_0, \dots, e_3$ be the standard basis of $K^4$, with $e_2$ the
  height direction, and write $e_{ij} = e_i \wedge e_j$. The plane
  $z = 0$ is spanned by $e_0$, $e_1$ and $e_3$. So at $z = 0$ every hinge
  $p_u \wedge p_v$ lies in
  $W_\Pi = \operatorname{span}(e_{01}, e_{03}, e_{13})$, the span of the
  lines of that plane. Split $\Lambda^2 K^4 = W' \oplus W_\Pi$ with
  $W' = \operatorname{span}(e_{02}, e_{12}, e_{23})$, and write a screw
  as $m_v = (m'_v, m^\Pi_v)$. The hinge condition
  $m_u - m_v \in \operatorname{span}(p_u \wedge p_v)$
  (\cref{def:hinge-constraint}) splits into $m'_u = m'_v$ and
  $m^\Pi_u - m^\Pi_v \in \operatorname{span}(p_u \wedge p_v)$. As $G$ is
  connected, the first condition makes $m'$ constant, which gives three
  dimensions.

  For the second, identify $W_\Pi = \Lambda^2 \hat\Pi$, where
  $\hat\Pi = \operatorname{span}(e_0, e_1, e_3)$, with the affine
  functions on $K^2$ by $a \wedge b \mapsto \det(a, b, \cdot)$, evaluated
  at $(x, y, 1)$. The line $p_u \wedge p_v$ becomes a nonzero affine
  function vanishing at $q_u$ and $q_v$. As $q_u \ne q_v$, its multiples
  are all the affine functions vanishing at these two points. So the
  second condition says that $h_u - h_v$ vanishes at $q_u$ and $q_v$,
  where $h_v$ is the affine function of $m^\Pi_v$. Its solutions are
  $F(q)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{fmlnote}
  \label{fmlnote:pencil-lifting-planes}
  The families of lifting planes range over the whole ambient set of
  bodies and are unconstrained off $V(G)$, as the infinitesimal motions
  of every framework are. Each body off $V(G)$ adds three to
  $\dim F(q)$ and six to the dimension of the motions in
  \cref{lem:pencil-flat-split}. These terms cancel in every rank.
\end{fmlnote}

\begin{lemma}[The lifting planes are the motions of a plane framework]
  \label{lem:pencil-lifting-planes-motions}
  \lean{Graph.finrank_infinitesimalMotions_ofNormals_pencilPicturePoint,
        Graph.finrank_span_rigidityRows_ofNormals_pencilPicturePoint}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-lifting-planes, lem:pencil-lifting-planes-dim,
        def:panel-hinge-framework, def:rigidity-matrix}
  Let $q$ be admissible, and let $P_q$ be the panel-hinge framework in
  the plane on $G$ whose panel normal at each body $v$ is
  $(x_v, y_v, 1)$ (\cref{def:panel-hinge-framework}, at $n = 2$). Then
  the infinitesimal motions of $P_q$ form a space isomorphic to $F(q)$,
  and $\operatorname{rank} R(P_q) = 3|V(G)| - \dim L(q)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:panel-support-extensor}
  The screw space of the plane is $K^3$. The hinge of $P_q$ at an edge
  $uv$ is the meet of the lines with normals $(x_u, y_u, 1)$ and
  $(x_v, y_v, 1)$ (\cref{def:panel-support-extensor}). It is a nonzero
  vector orthogonal to both normals, since $q_u \ne q_v$. So a relative
  screw is a multiple of the hinge exactly when it is orthogonal to both
  normals, which is the condition defining $F(q)$. The rank follows by
  rank--nullity and \cref{lem:pencil-lifting-planes-dim}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The flat lower bound on the lifting space]
  \label{lem:pencil-lifting-space-deficiency}
  \lean{Graph.three_add_deficiency_le_finrank_liftingSpace}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-lifting-space, def:D-deficiency}
  Let $G$ have at least one body and let $q$ be admissible. Then
  $\dim L(q) \ge 3 + \operatorname{def}_2(G)$, where
  $\operatorname{def}_2(G) = \max_P\,[3(|P| - 1) - 2d(P)]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:pencil-lifting-planes-motions, lem:pencil-lifting-planes-dim,
        lem:relative-deficiency-rank-bound}
  By \cref{lem:pencil-lifting-planes-motions} and
  \cref{lem:pencil-lifting-planes-dim}, it suffices to show that the
  motions of $P_q$ have dimension at least
  $3 + \operatorname{def}_2(G)$. Each body of $P_q$ has the screw space
  $K^3$, and each hinge is nonzero, as adjacent picture points differ.
  Let $P$ be a partition of $V(G)$. The screw assignments that are
  constant on each part form a space of dimension $3|P|$. On it the edges
  inside a part impose no condition, and each crossing edge imposes at
  most two. So the motions have dimension at least
  $3|P| - 2d(P) = 3 + [3(|P| - 1) - 2d(P)]$ for every $P$, and hence at
  least $3 + \operatorname{def}_2(G)$. This is the relative deficiency
  bound at $D = 3$ (\cref{lem:relative-deficiency-rank-bound}).
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The flat rank]
  \label{thm:pencil-flat-rank}
  \lean{Graph.finrank_span_rigidityRows_ofNormals_flat,
        Graph.finrank_span_rigidityRows_ofNormals_flat_le,
        Graph.finrank_span_rigidityRows_ofNormals_flat_eq_iff}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-x0-attains, def:pencil-lifting-space, def:D-deficiency}
  Let $G$ be connected and let $q$ be admissible. Then the rank
  $\operatorname{rank} R(q, 0)$ of the flat configuration
  (\cref{def:pencil-x0-attains}) is exactly
  $6|V(G)| - 3 - \dim L(q)$. Hence
  $\operatorname{rank} R(q, 0) \le 6(|V(G)| - 1) - \operatorname{def}_2(G)$,
  with equality if and only if $\dim L(q) = 3 + \operatorname{def}_2(G)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:pencil-flat-split, lem:pencil-lifting-planes-dim,
        lem:pencil-config-point-join-rank,
        lem:pencil-lifting-space-deficiency}
  The rank of $(q, 0)$ is that of $F_{(q,0)}$
  (\cref{lem:pencil-config-point-join-rank}). Its motions have dimension
  $3 + \dim F(q) = 3 + \dim L(q)$ (\cref{lem:pencil-flat-split},
  \cref{lem:pencil-lifting-planes-dim}). By rank--nullity in the
  $6|V(G)|$-dimensional space of screw assignments, the rank is
  $6|V(G)| - 3 - \dim L(q)$. The bound and its equality case follow from
  \cref{lem:pencil-lifting-space-deficiency}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[When the flat configuration has the deficiency rank]
  \label{cor:pencil-flat-attains}
  \lean{Graph.finrank_liftingSpace_eq_three_add_deficiency_three_iff,
        Graph.x0Attains_of_finrank_liftingSpace_le}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:pencil-flat-rank, def:pencil-x0-attains}
  Let $G$ be connected and let $q$ be admissible. The flat configuration
  $(q, 0)$ has rank $6(|V(G)| - 1) - \operatorname{def}_3(G)$ if and only
  if $\dim L(q) = 3 + \operatorname{def}_3(G)$, and this holds if and
  only if equality holds in \cref{lem:pencil-lifting-space-deficiency} at
  $q$ and $\operatorname{def}_2(G) = \operatorname{def}_3(G)$. If
  $\dim L(q) \le 3 + \operatorname{def}_3(G)$, then the general
  configuration of $G$ attains.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:pencil-lifting-space-deficiency, lem:deficiency-antitone,
        lem:pencil-x0-one-witness, def:pencil-main-picture}
  The first equivalence is the exact rank of \cref{thm:pencil-flat-rank}.
  By \cref{lem:pencil-lifting-space-deficiency} and
  \cref{lem:deficiency-antitone}, every admissible picture $q'$ has
  \[
    \dim L(q') \;\ge\; 3 + \operatorname{def}_2(G) \;\ge\;
    3 + \operatorname{def}_3(G).
  \]
  At $q' = q$ this gives the second equivalence.

  Now suppose that $\dim L(q) \le 3 + \operatorname{def}_3(G)$. Then
  $\dim L(q') \ge \dim L(q)$ at every admissible picture $q'$, so $q$ is
  a main picture (\cref{def:pencil-main-picture}). By the exact rank, the
  flat configuration $(q, 0)$ has rank at least
  $6(|V(G)| - 1) - \operatorname{def}_3(G)$, and its height $0$ lies in
  $L(q)$. So it is a witness for \cref{lem:pencil-x0-one-witness}, and
  the general configuration of $G$ attains.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[The flat configuration when the lifting space has dimension three]
  \label{cor:pencil-flat-x0}
  \lean{Graph.x0Attains_of_finrank_liftingSpace_eq_three,
        Graph.finrank_span_rigidityRows_ofNormals_flat_of_finrank_eq_three}
  \leanok
  \uses{cor:pencil-flat-attains, lem:pencil-lifting-space-affine}
  Let $G$ be connected, and suppose that $\dim L(q) = 3$ at some
  admissible picture $q$, so that $\ell_0 = 3$
  (\cref{lem:pencil-lifting-space-affine}). Then
  $\operatorname{def}_2(G) = \operatorname{def}_3(G) = 0$, the flat
  configuration at every admissible $q$ with $\dim L(q) = 3$ has rank
  $6(|V(G)| - 1)$, and the general configuration of $G$ attains.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:pencil-flat-rank, lem:pencil-lifting-space-deficiency,
        lem:deficiency-antitone}
  By \cref{lem:pencil-lifting-space-deficiency},
  $3 + \operatorname{def}_2(G) \le 3$, so
  $\operatorname{def}_2(G) \le 0$. Both deficiencies are nonnegative,
  and $\operatorname{def}_3(G) \le \operatorname{def}_2(G)$
  (\cref{lem:deficiency-antitone}), so both vanish. By
  \cref{thm:pencil-flat-rank} the flat configuration then has rank
  $6|V(G)| - 6$, and \cref{cor:pencil-flat-attains} applies.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Jackson and Jord\'an's equality}
\label{sec:main-component-jj}

In this subsection we prove the equality of Jackson and Jord\'an
(\cref{thm:pencil-jj-equality}). Let $G$ be simple, with at least three
members in every closed neighbourhood. At a general picture the lower
bound $\dim L(q) \ge 3 + \operatorname{def}_2(G)$ of
\cref{lem:pencil-lifting-space-deficiency} is then an equality. With the
flat rank, the equality settles the case of equal deficiencies
(\cref{cor:pencil-jj-flat}). The contraction, ear and split-off steps use
it at the smaller graphs they compare with $G$.

The equality is Jackson and Jord\'an's theorem on pin-collinear
body-and-pin frameworks~\cite{jacksonJordan2008pin}. Such a framework
consists of rigid bodies in the real plane, with each pair of adjacent
bodies joined by a pin and the pins of each body on one line. In the
plane a panel is a line and a hinge is a point, so these are Katoh and
Tanigawa's panel-hinge frameworks at
$d = 2$~\cite[pp.~648--649]{katohTanigawa2011}. Jackson and Jord\'an
prove the following for a simple graph $G$ without isolated vertices.
Choose the line of each body generically, and pin adjacent bodies where
their lines meet. Then the infinitesimal motions form a space of
dimension $3 + \operatorname{def}_2(G)$. The plane framework $P_q$ of
\cref{lem:pencil-lifting-planes-motions} is a framework of this kind, in
which the body $v$ lies on the line with normal $(x_v, y_v, 1)$. Its
motions are the lifting planes, and these form a space of dimension
$\dim L(q)$ (\cref{lem:pencil-lifting-planes-dim}). So over the reals,
their theorem at a generic picture is our equality.

Jackson and Jord\'an's argument is specific to the reals. Their
genericity is algebraic independence over $\Q$, and their proof moves
points within small Euclidean neighbourhoods. We need the equality over
every infinite field, and KT Theorem~5.6 holds over every infinite field
(\cref{thm:theorem-55-6-rows}). So we derive the equality from its case
$d = 2$. That case gives a panel-hinge framework on $G$ in the plane of
rank $3(|V(G)| - 1) - \operatorname{def}_2(G)$. The rank does not drop
when the normals move to a general point. It does not change when each
normal is divided by its third coordinate (\cref{lem:pencil-jj-rescale}).
So at a general picture, $P_q$ has at least this rank
(\cref{lem:pencil-jj-chart}). As
$\operatorname{rank} R(P_q) = 3|V(G)| - \dim L(q)$, the bound reads
$\dim L(q) \le 3 + \operatorname{def}_2(G)$.

\begin{lemma}[Rescaling the panel normals]
  \label{lem:pencil-jj-rescale}
  \lean{CombinatorialRigidity.Molecular.panelSupportExtensor_smul_right,
        CombinatorialRigidity.Molecular.PanelHingeFramework.supportExtensor_ofNormals_smul,
        CombinatorialRigidity.Molecular.PanelHingeFramework.infinitesimalMotions_ofNormals_smul,
        CombinatorialRigidity.Molecular.PanelHingeFramework.finrank_span_rigidityRows_ofNormals_smul}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:panel-hinge-framework, def:panel-support-extensor,
        def:rigidity-matrix}
  Let $(n_v)$ be panel normals and $(c_v)$ nonzero scalars. Then at every
  edge $e$ with ends $u$ and $v$, the panel-hinge framework on $G$ with
  normals $c_v n_v$ has the hinge $c_u c_v\, C(e)$, where $C(e)$ is its
  hinge at the normals $n_v$. In particular the two frameworks have the
  same infinitesimal motions, and their rigidity matrices have the same
  rank.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:hinge-constraint, lem:span-rigidityRows-eq-of-motions-eq}
  The support extensor is linear in each of its two normals
  (\cref{def:panel-support-extensor}). A nonzero multiple of a hinge
  spans the same line, and the hinge constraint depends only on that line
  (\cref{def:hinge-constraint}). Equal motion spaces give equal row spans
  (\cref{lem:span-rigidityRows-eq-of-motions-eq}).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The planar case of KT Theorem 5.6, in the chart]
  \label{lem:pencil-jj-chart}
  \lean{Graph.exists_mvPolynomial_le_finrank_span_rigidityRows_pencilPicturePoint}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:theorem-55-6-rows, lem:pencil-lifting-planes-motions}
  Let $K$ be infinite and let $G$ be simple with at least two bodies.
  There is a nonzero polynomial $P$ in the picture coordinates such that
  at every picture $q$ with $P(q) \ne 0$ the plane framework $P_q$ of
  \cref{lem:pencil-lifting-planes-motions}, with panel normal
  $(x_v, y_v, 1)$ at each body, has
  $\operatorname{rank} R(P_q) \ge 3(|V(G)| - 1) -
  \operatorname{def}_2(G)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:rank-polynomial-of-le-finrank, def:panel-general-position,
        lem:general-position-support-nonzero, lem:pencil-jj-rescale}
  By \cref{thm:theorem-55-6-rows} at $n = 2$ ($D = 3$), $G$ has a
  panel-hinge realization of rank
  $3(|V(G)| - 1) - \operatorname{def}_2(G)$. As $K$ is infinite, three
  nonzero polynomials in the normals have a common non-root $n$: the rank
  polynomial of this realization
  (\cref{lem:rank-polynomial-of-le-finrank}), a polynomial whose non-roots
  are in general position (\cref{def:panel-general-position}), and the
  product of the third coordinates of the normals. Dividing each normal
  $n_v$ by its third coordinate gives the normals $(x_v, y_v, 1)$ of a
  picture $q'$. The framework at $q'$ has the rank of the framework at
  $n$, at least $3(|V(G)| - 1) - \operatorname{def}_2(G)$
  (\cref{lem:pencil-jj-rescale}). Its hinges are nonzero
  (\cref{lem:general-position-support-nonzero}). So the rank polynomial
  of the framework at $q'$, composed with the chart
  $q \mapsto (x_v, y_v, 1)$, is nonzero at $q'$. It is the required $P$.
\end{proof}

\begin{fmlnote}
  \label{fmlnote:pencil-jj-embed-edges}
  The formal case $d = 2$ of KT Theorem~5.6 assumes a supply of spare
  edge labels (\cref{rem:fresh-edge-supply}), and so does
  \cref{lem:pencil-jj-chart}. The equality mentions no edge label, so
  its formal proof first relabels the edges into a larger label set,
  which has spare labels (\cref{lem:pencil-jj-embed-edges}). The equality
  therefore assumes nothing about the labels.
\end{fmlnote}

\begin{lemma}[Relabelling the edges]
  \label{lem:pencil-jj-embed-edges}
  \lean{Graph.embedEdges, Graph.vertexSet_embedEdges, Graph.embedEdges_isLink,
        Graph.edgeSet_embedEdges, Graph.closedNbhd_embedEdges, Graph.Simple.embedEdges,
        Graph.liftingSpace_embedEdges, Graph.finrank_liftingSpace_embedEdges,
        Graph.isAdmissiblePicture_embedEdges_iff, Graph.isMainPicture_embedEdges_iff,
        Graph.deficiency_embedEdges}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-lifting-space, def:pencil-admissible-picture,
        def:pencil-main-picture, def:D-deficiency}
  Let $G'$ be $G$ with its edges relabelled injectively into a larger set
  of labels. Then $G'$ is simple if $G$ is, and $G'$ has the lifting
  spaces $L(q)$, the admissible and main pictures, and the deficiencies
  $\operatorname{def}_n$ of $G$, for every $n$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The bodies, the pairs of adjacent bodies and hence the closed
  neighbourhoods are unchanged. A partition crosses exactly the images of
  the edges it crossed before.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Jackson and Jord\'an's equality]
  \label{thm:pencil-jj-equality}
  \lean{Graph.exists_mvPolynomial_finrank_liftingSpace_eq,
        Graph.IsMainPicture.finrank_liftingSpace_eq,
        Graph.exists_isMainPicture_finrank_liftingSpace_eq,
        Graph.closedNbhd_subset_vertexSet}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-lifting-space, def:pencil-main-picture,
        def:D-deficiency}
  Let $K$ be infinite and let $G$ be simple, with at least one body and
  at least three members in every closed neighbourhood. There is a
  nonzero polynomial $P$ in the picture coordinates such that every
  picture $q$ with $P(q) \ne 0$ is a main picture with
  $\dim L(q) = 3 + \operatorname{def}_2(G)$. Consequently
  $\ell_0 = 3 + \operatorname{def}_2(G)$: every main picture has
  $\dim L(q) = 3 + \operatorname{def}_2(G)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:pencil-jj-chart, lem:pencil-jj-embed-edges,
        lem:pencil-x0-main-picture-open, lem:pencil-lifting-planes-motions,
        lem:pencil-lifting-space-deficiency}
  Let $P$ be the product of two nonzero polynomials. The non-roots of the
  first are main pictures (\cref{lem:pencil-x0-main-picture-open}). The
  second is the polynomial of \cref{lem:pencil-jj-chart}, which needs two
  bodies. Here $G$ has at least three, since a closed neighbourhood of a
  body lies in $V(G)$. (The formal proof applies \cref{lem:pencil-jj-chart}
  to $G$ with its edges relabelled, \cref{lem:pencil-jj-embed-edges}.)

  Let $q$ be a non-root of $P$. The plane framework $P_q$ has rank at
  least $3(|V(G)| - 1) - \operatorname{def}_2(G)$, and exactly
  $3|V(G)| - \dim L(q)$ (\cref{lem:pencil-lifting-planes-motions}). So
  $\dim L(q) \le 3 + \operatorname{def}_2(G)$. The reverse inequality is
  \cref{lem:pencil-lifting-space-deficiency}. As $q$ is a main picture,
  $\ell_0 = \dim L(q) = 3 + \operatorname{def}_2(G)$
  (\cref{def:pencil-main-picture}).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[The general configuration attains when the two deficiencies agree]
  \label{cor:pencil-jj-flat}
  \lean{Graph.x0Attains_of_deficiency_two_eq_three}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-x0-attains, def:D-deficiency}
  Let $K$ be infinite and let $G$ be connected and simple, with at least
  three members in every closed neighbourhood. If
  $\operatorname{def}_2(G) = \operatorname{def}_3(G)$, then the general
  configuration of $G$ attains (\cref{def:pencil-x0-attains}).
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{thm:pencil-jj-equality, cor:pencil-flat-attains}
  By \cref{thm:pencil-jj-equality}, some main picture $q$ has
  $\dim L(q) = 3 + \operatorname{def}_2(G) = 3 + \operatorname{def}_3(G)$.
  So the general configuration of $G$ attains
  (\cref{cor:pencil-flat-attains}).
\end{proof}

\subsection{Cut vertices and bridges}
\label{sec:main-component-cut}

This subsection and the six after it prove the steps of the induction,
other than the case of equal deficiencies (\cref{cor:pencil-jj-flat}).
In each step we show that the general configuration of a graph $G$
satisfying the standing hypotheses (\cref{def:pencil-x0-standing})
attains, assuming the same of one or two smaller graphs (of none, for a
cycle). This subsection proves the first two steps: at a cut vertex, and
along a chain of bridges, a path of bridges whose interior bodies have
degree two. The shape that the steps share is plainest in these two,
and we describe it first.

Write
\[
  \operatorname{tgt}(G) \;=\; 6(|V(G)| - 1) - \operatorname{def}(\tilde G)
\]
for the deficiency rank of $G$ (\cref{def:pencil-rank-hypothesis}, with
$D = 6$), and call it the \emph{target} of $G$. We end every step in the
same way. We exhibit one configuration of $G$ over a main picture of
$G$, with its heights in the lifting space, whose rank is at least
$\operatorname{tgt}(G)$. Then the general configuration of $G$ attains
(\cref{lem:pencil-x0-one-witness}). At a cut vertex and along a chain of
bridges we find this configuration $(q, z)$ in three parts.
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \emph{The picture.} As $K$ is infinite, a product of nonzero
    polynomials has a non-root. So we can take $q$ off the zero sets of
    finitely many nonzero polynomials in the picture coordinates. One is
    the polynomial of \cref{lem:pencil-x0-main-picture-open} for $G$, so
    that $q$ is a main picture of $G$. The others are the polynomials of
    the attainment at the smaller graphs $G'$
    (\cref{def:pencil-x0-attains}). Then for each $G'$, the heights in
    $L_{G'}(q)$ at which $G'$ has its target contain a nonempty
    Zariski-open subset of $L_{G'}(q)$.
  \item \emph{The height.} Here each smaller graph $G'$ is a subgraph of
    $G$. The rank of $G'$ depends only on the points of its own bodies.
    So at a height $z$ of $G$ it equals the rank of $G'$ at the
    restriction of $z$ to $V(G')$ (\cref{lem:pencil-lifting-restrict},
    \cref{lem:pencil-rank-congr}). If the restriction maps $L_G(q)$ onto
    $L_{G'}(q)$, the heights $z \in L_G(q)$ at which $G'$ has its target
    therefore contain a nonempty Zariski-open subset of $L_G(q)$. Two
    such subsets meet (\cref{lem:pencil-x0-fibre-intersection}).
  \item \emph{The count.} A rank-addition lemma bounds the rank of
    $(q, z)$ below through the ranks of the smaller graphs. A deficiency
    count shows that this bound is at least $\operatorname{tgt}(G)$.
\end{enumerate}

The later steps choose the picture and make the count in the same way,
and vary how the height is found; each subsection says how. Often two
open conditions still meet in one linear space, though not always in
$L_G(q)$. One of them may ask that the hinges of an ear be independent.
Some steps also move some bodies, with their heights, through a
polynomial family of configurations of $G$, and we take a general member
of the family. In three steps no two open conditions need to meet: the
cycle (\cref{thm:pencil-x0-cycle}), the split-off step
(\cref{thm:pencil-x0-splitoff}), and the contraction at a core of planar
deficiency zero (\cref{thm:pencil-x0-contract-rigid}).

At a cut vertex and along a chain of bridges, the restriction maps
$L_G(q)$ onto the lifting spaces of the two sides, at every picture $q$.
Each side meets the rest of $G$ at one body: the cut vertex, or the end
of the path in that side. A height of the side extends to a height of
$G$ by the affine function with which it agrees on the closed
neighbourhood of that body (\cref{lem:pencil-cut-fibre},
\cref{lem:pencil-bridge-fibre}). At a cut vertex the ranks of the two
sides add (\cref{lem:block-rank-cut-vertex}), and so do their
deficiencies (\cref{lem:deficiency-cut-vertex}). Along a chain of
bridges, each bridge adds at least $5$ to the rank
(\cref{lem:block-rank-cut}) and exactly $1$ to the deficiency
(\cref{lem:cut-edge-decomposition}).

We first record the hypotheses of the steps, and the two facts that let
us read the rank of a subgraph at a height of $G$.

\begin{definition}[The standing hypotheses]
  \label{def:pencil-x0-standing}
  \lean{Graph.IsX0Graph, Graph.three_le_ncard_closedNbhd,
    Graph.IsX0Graph.three_le_ncard_closedNbhd}
  \leanok
  A graph $G$ satisfies the \emph{standing hypotheses} if it is finite,
  simple and connected, with minimum degree at least two. Then every
  closed neighbourhood of $G$ has at least three members: a body of
  degree $d$ in a simple graph has $d$ distinct neighbours.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Heights restrict to a subgraph]
  \label{lem:pencil-lifting-restrict}
  \lean{Graph.liftingRestrict, Graph.liftingRestrict_apply,
    Graph.liftingRestrict_mem_liftingSpace,
    CombinatorialRigidity.Molecular.pencilConfigPoint_liftingRestrict,
    CombinatorialRigidity.Molecular.restrictPoly,
    CombinatorialRigidity.Molecular.eval_restrictPoly}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-lifting-space, def:pencil-configuration}
  Let $G' \le G$ and let $q$ be any picture. The height that agrees with
  $z \in L_G(q)$ on $V(G')$ and vanishes elsewhere lies in
  $L_{G'}(q)$, and its configuration points agree with those of $z$ at
  the bodies of $G'$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The restriction agrees with $z$ on $V(G')$. A closed neighbourhood in
  $G'$ lies in the closed neighbourhood of the same body in $G$, where $z$
  agrees with an affine function of the picture. So the restriction
  agrees with that affine function on it too. The configuration points
  at a body of $G'$ read only its picture point and its height.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The rank reads only the bodies of the graph]
  \label{lem:pencil-rank-congr}
  \lean{CombinatorialRigidity.Molecular.PanelHingeFramework.finrank_span_rigidityRows_ofNormals_congr}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:panel-hinge-framework, def:rigidity-matrix}
  The rank of the rigidity matrix of the panel-hinge framework on $G$
  depends only on the panel normals at the bodies of $G$, and not on
  which end of each edge is recorded first.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:panel-support-extensor, def:hinge-constraint,
        lem:span-rigidityRows-eq-of-motions-eq}
  The hinge at an edge is built from the normals at its two ends, and
  swapping them negates it (\cref{def:panel-support-extensor}); the
  hinge constraint reads only the line it spans
  (\cref{def:hinge-constraint}). So the motion spaces agree, and equal
  motion spaces give equal row spans
  (\cref{lem:span-rigidityRows-eq-of-motions-eq}).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The heights at a cut vertex]
  \label{lem:pencil-cut-fibre}
  \lean{Graph.exists_liftingRestrict_eq_of_cutVertex}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:pencil-lifting-restrict}
  Let $G = G_1 \cup G_2$ with $V(G_1) \cap V(G_2) = \{v\}$ and disjoint
  edge sets. At every picture $q$, both restriction maps
  $L_G(q) \to L_{G_i}(q)$ (\cref{lem:pencil-lifting-restrict}) are onto.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Given $z^1 \in L_{G_1}(q)$, extend it across $V(G_2)$ by the affine
  function of its closed neighbourhood at $v$. Every closed
  neighbourhood of $G$ other than $N_G[v]$ lies in one side, and
  $N_G[v] = N_1[v] \cup N_2[v]$ carries that affine function on both, so
  the extension lies in $L_G(q)$ and restricts to $z^1$. The other side
  is symmetric.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[A cut vertex]
  \label{thm:pencil-x0-cut}
  \lean{Graph.X0Attains.of_cutVertex}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-x0-attains, def:pencil-x0-standing}
  Let $K$ be infinite and let $G$ satisfy the standing hypotheses
  (\cref{def:pencil-x0-standing}), with $V(G) = V_1 \cup V_2$,
  $V_1 \cap V_2 = \{v\}$ and every edge of $G$ inside $V_1$ or inside
  $V_2$. If the general configurations of $G[V_1]$ and $G[V_2]$ attain,
  then the general configuration of $G$ attains.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:pencil-cut-fibre, lem:block-rank-cut-vertex,
        lem:deficiency-cut-vertex, lem:pencil-lifting-restrict,
        lem:pencil-rank-congr, lem:pencil-x0-fibre-intersection,
        lem:pencil-x0-main-picture-open, lem:pencil-x0-one-witness}
  We follow the three parts above. Take a picture $q$ off the zero sets
  of the polynomials of the attainment at $G[V_1]$ and at $G[V_2]$, and
  of the main-picture polynomial of $G$
  (\cref{lem:pencil-x0-main-picture-open}). For $i = 1, 2$ some
  polynomial $R_i$ in the heights is nonzero somewhere on
  $L_{G[V_i]}(q)$, and $G[V_i]$ has its target at every height of
  $L_{G[V_i]}(q)$ off the zero set of $R_i$. Read at the restriction to
  $V_i$, $R_i$ is a polynomial in the heights of $G$
  (\cref{lem:pencil-lifting-restrict}). It is nonzero somewhere on
  $L_G(q)$, since the restriction is onto (\cref{lem:pencil-cut-fibre}).
  So some $z \in L_G(q)$ makes both nonzero
  (\cref{lem:pencil-x0-fibre-intersection}).

  At $(q, z)$ the rank of each side is its target
  (\cref{lem:pencil-rank-congr}), and the rank of $G$ is their sum
  (\cref{lem:block-rank-cut-vertex}). The deficiencies add
  (\cref{lem:deficiency-cut-vertex}), and $|V_1| + |V_2| = |V(G)| + 1$, so
  \[
    \operatorname{tgt}(G[V_1]) + \operatorname{tgt}(G[V_2]) \;=\;
    \operatorname{tgt}(G).
  \]
  Hence \cref{lem:pencil-x0-one-witness} applies.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The heights along a chain of bridges]
  \label{lem:pencil-bridge-fibre}
  \lean{CombinatorialRigidity.Molecular.pathVertex,
    CombinatorialRigidity.Molecular.pathVertex_zero,
    CombinatorialRigidity.Molecular.pathVertex_eq_or_exists,
    Graph.exists_liftingRestrict_eq_of_bridgePath}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:pencil-lifting-restrict}
  Let $G$ be $G_1 \sqcup G_2$ plus a path $a - x_1 - \cdots - x_k - b$
  ($k \ge 0$ new vertices), with $a \in V(G_1)$, $b \in V(G_2)$, and
  every other edge of $G$ inside $G_1$ or inside $G_2$. At every
  picture $q$, both restriction maps $L_G(q) \to L_{G_i}(q)$ are onto.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Given $z^1 \in L_{G_1}(q)$, take an affine function $h$ agreeing
  with $z^1$ on the closed neighbourhood $N_{G_1}[a]$
  (\cref{def:pencil-lifting-space}), and extend $z^1$ by $h$ on every
  body outside $G_1$. The body $a$ is the only body of $G_1$ with an
  edge leaving $G_1$: the path's own bodies and $b$ lie outside $G_1$,
  so only the path's first edge, at $a$, can cross. So the closed
  neighbourhood in $G$ of a body of $G_1$ other than $a$ lies in $G_1$,
  where the extension is $z^1$; the closed neighbourhood of $a$, or of
  a body outside $G_1$, meets $G_1$ only inside $N_{G_1}[a]$, so the
  extension equals $h$ on all of it. The extension is therefore affine
  on every closed neighbourhood of $G$: it lies in $L_G(q)$ and
  restricts to $z^1$. The other restriction is symmetric.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[A chain of bridges]
  \label{thm:pencil-x0-bridge}
  \lean{Graph.X0Attains.of_bridgePath,
    CombinatorialRigidity.Molecular.pathVertex_cases,
    CombinatorialRigidity.Molecular.pathVertex_eq_of_val_eq_succ,
    CombinatorialRigidity.Molecular.pathVertex_rev,
    CombinatorialRigidity.Molecular.pathVertex_mem_union_image_iff,
    Graph.cutEdges_union_image_of_bridgePath,
    Graph.deficiency_induce_union_range_of_bridgePath,
    CombinatorialRigidity.Molecular.BodyHingeFramework.add_le_finrank_span_rigidityRows_induce_union_range_of_bridgePath}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-x0-attains, def:pencil-x0-standing}
  Let $K$ be infinite and let $G$ satisfy the standing hypotheses. Let
  $V(G)$ be the disjoint union of $V_1$, $V_2$ and path bodies
  $x_1, \dots, x_k$ ($k \ge 0$), with a path $a - x_1 - \cdots - x_k - b$
  from $a \in V_1$ to $b \in V_2$, every other edge of $G$ lying inside
  $V_1$ or inside $V_2$. If the general configurations of $G[V_1]$ and
  $G[V_2]$ attain, then the general configuration of $G$ attains.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:pencil-bridge-fibre, lem:block-rank-cut, lem:cut-edge-decomposition,
        lem:pencil-lifting-restrict, lem:pencil-rank-congr,
        lem:pencil-x0-fibre-intersection, lem:pencil-x0-main-picture-open,
        lem:pencil-x0-one-witness}
  We find $q$ and $z$ as in the proof of \cref{thm:pencil-x0-cut}, with
  \cref{lem:pencil-bridge-fibre} for the restrictions. For $G[V_2]$ we
  apply it from $b$, along the reversed path. We count the ranks and the
  deficiencies directly, rather than apply the step one bridge at a
  time. That would ask a graph with one path body as its only body to
  attain, and no picture of such a graph is admissible.

  Write $S_j = V_1 \cup \{x_1, \dots, x_j\}$. Every edge off the path
  lies inside $V_1$ or inside $V_2$, so the only edge of $G$ leaving
  $S_j$ is the path edge from $S_j$ to the next path body, or to $b$
  when $j = k$. The last of these edges is a bridge between $G[S_k]$ and
  $G[V_2]$. It adds $D - 1 = 5$ to the rank (\cref{lem:block-rank-cut};
  every hinge is nonzero at an admissible picture) and $1$ to the
  deficiency (\cref{lem:cut-edge-decomposition}). Inside $G[S_{j+1}]$ the
  path body $x_{j+1}$ is joined to $S_j$ by a single edge. By the same
  two lemmas, it adds at least $5$ to the rank and exactly $1$ to the
  deficiency. Compared with the two sides together, the term
  $6(|V(G)| - 1)$ is larger by $6(k + 1)$ and the deficiency by $k + 1$,
  so
  \[
    \operatorname{tgt}(G) \;=\; \operatorname{tgt}(G[V_1]) +
    \operatorname{tgt}(G[V_2]) + 5(k + 1).
  \]
