$\rho$ and $\Lambda$ as in \cref{sec:main-component-chain}, we prove the
inequality $(\star)$ of \cref{sec:main-component-short} at $k = 1$ and
at $k = 2$. Its derivation there holds for every $k$.

The hypothesis on the merged deficiency places the planes of the two
ends. At a solution $(z, h)$ of the lifting system of $G[V_1]$
(\cref{def:pencil-weighted-lifting-system}), let $\pi_a$ and $\pi_b$ be
the graphs of $h_a$ and $h_b$, the planes of $a$ and $b$
(\cref{sec:main-component-short}). As $z_b = h_b \cdot (q_b, 1)$, the
point $p_b$ lies on $\pi_a$ exactly when $h_a - h_b$ vanishes at
$(q_b, 1)$, and likewise for $p_a$ and $\pi_b$. If both held, $z$ would be
a height of $G[V_1] + ab$, the graph $G[V_1]$ with the edge $ab$ added.
Adding $ab$ lowers the planar deficiency by at least two
(\cref{lem:splitoff-deficiency-merged}). So at a general picture the
lifting space of $G[V_1] + ab$ is at least two smaller than that of
$G[V_1]$ (\cref{thm:pencil-jj-equality},
\cref{lem:pencil-lifting-space-deficiency}). The two conditions are
therefore independent on the solutions for $G[V_1]$
(\cref{lem:pencil-flag-genericity}). At a general solution,
$p_b \notin \pi_a$ and $p_a \notin \pi_b$.

\emph{One interior body.} Here $(\star)$ at $k = 1$ asks only that
$\dim(\rho + \Lambda) \ge 2$, as
$\operatorname{def}(\tilde G[V_1]) \le \operatorname{def}(\tilde G)$. The
two joins of the ear give this at every admissible picture, where the
picture points of $a$, $x_1$ and $b$ are not collinear. It remains to
find the height. A solution for $G$ restricts to one for $G[V_1]$ whose
planes $\pi_a$ and $\pi_b$ both contain the point of $x_1$, so $h_a - h_b$
vanishes at $(q_{x_1}, 1)$. At a fixed picture this is a linear condition
on the solutions for $G[V_1]$, which the open set where $G[V_1]$ has its
target need not meet. So we choose the picture of $x_1$ together with the
solution, as a point of their incidence. Such a point can be found off
the zero sets of a polynomial in the solution and of one in the picture
of $x_1$ (\cref{lem:pencil-one-ear-incidence}). For this, $h_a - h_b$ must
range over a space of affine functions of dimension at least two, as the
independence above gives. The result is the configuration of the step
(\cref{lem:pencil-one-ear-base}). It also has $p_b \notin \pi_a$ and
$p_a \notin \pi_b$, which the step with two interior bodies uses.

\emph{Two interior bodies.} As with three and four interior bodies, we
count against a smaller graph, here $G_1$. It has the one-body ear
$a - x_1 - b$ on $V_1$, and its deficiency is at most that of $G$
(\cref{lem:splitoff-deficiency-reuse}). Let $W$ be the span of $\rho$ and
the two joins of its ear. Where $G[V_1]$ and $G_1$ have their targets, the
ear rank formula at $G_1$ (\cref{sec:main-component-chain}) gives
$\dim W$, and $(\star)$ asks for at most one dimension more. We take the
configuration of $G_1$ from the one-body step, with $G_1$ at its target
too. There the point $y$ of $x_1$ lies on both $\pi_a$ and $\pi_b$, and
each end's point lies off the other end's plane. So the two planes meet in
a line through $y$. With $u_0$ a direction of this line, of last
coordinate $0$, the points $p_a$, $y$, $u_0$ and $p_b$ are linearly
independent. Their six joins span the screw space
(\cref{lem:pencil-tetrahedron}), and $W$ contains two of them,
$p_a \wedge y$ and $y \wedge p_b$. Unless $W$ is the whole screw space,
one of the other four lies outside $W$. We then place $x_1$ on $\pi_a$
and $x_2$ on $\pi_b$, on lines through $y$ chosen for that join, and the
span gains a dimension (\cref{lem:pencil-insertion-two}). The heights
follow the points through the ear data (\cref{def:pencil-ear-data}). A
general ear datum gives the configuration of $G$, as in the second stage
of \cref{sec:main-component-short}.

We prove the independence and the incidence first, then the
configuration and the step with one interior body, and then the
insertion and the step with two.

In the steps, the graphs $G'$ and $H$ of the next lemma are $G[V_1]$ and
$G[V_1] + ab$.

\begin{lemma}[The incidence functionals at the two ends are independent]
  \label{lem:pencil-flag-genericity}
  \lean{CombinatorialRigidity.Molecular.planeDiff,
        Graph.two_le_finrank_map_planeDiff}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-weighted-lifting-system, def:pencil-lifting-space,
        def:pencil-admissible-picture}
  Let $G'$ and $H$ be graphs on the same bodies, and $a, b$ two bodies of
  $G'$, such that every closed neighbourhood in $H$ lies in the closed
  neighbourhood of the same body in $G'$, except that the one of $a$ may
  also contain $b$ and the one of $b$ may also contain $a$. Let $q$ be
  admissible for $G'$, with $\dim L_H(q) + 2 \le \dim L_{G'}(q)$. Then on
  the solutions $(z, h)$ of the lifting system of $G'$ at $q$
  (\cref{def:pencil-weighted-lifting-system}), the two linear functionals
  \[
    (z, h) \mapsto (h_a - h_b) \cdot (q_a, 1), \qquad
    (z, h) \mapsto (h_a - h_b) \cdot (q_b, 1)
  \]
  are linearly independent.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:pencil-x0-one-witness}
  At a common zero $(z, h)$ of the two functionals, $h_a$ and $h_b$ take
  the same values at $q_a$ and at $q_b$. As $z_a = h_a \cdot (q_a, 1)$ and
  $z_b = h_b \cdot (q_b, 1)$, the height $z$ agrees with $h_a$ on the
  closed neighbourhood of $a$ in $G'$ together with $b$, and with $h_b$ on
  that of $b$ together with $a$. At every other body $v$, the closed
  neighbourhood in $H$ lies in the one in $G'$, on which $z$ agrees with
  $h_v$. So $z \in L_H(q)$. At a picture admissible for $G'$ a solution is
  determined by its heights, and the solutions form a space of dimension
  $\dim L_{G'}(q)$ (the proof of \cref{lem:pencil-x0-one-witness}). So the
  common zeros of the two functionals form a space of dimension at most
  $\dim L_H(q) \le \dim L_{G'}(q) - 2$, and the two are independent.
\end{proof}

When we apply the next lemma, $L$ is the space of solutions of the
lifting system of $G[V_1]$ at a picture, and $D$ maps a solution
$(z, h)$ to $h_a - h_b$.

\begin{lemma}[An incidence meets two open conditions]
  \label{lem:pencil-one-ear-incidence}
  \lean{CombinatorialRigidity.Molecular.exists_incidence}
  \leanok
  Let $K$ be infinite, let $L$ be a linear subspace of $K^\sigma$ for a
  finite index set $\sigma$, and let $D \colon K^\sigma \to K^3$ be linear
  with $\dim D(L) \ge 2$; read $D(z)$ as the affine function
  $q \mapsto D(z) \cdot (q, 1)$ on $K^2$. Let $A$ be a polynomial in the
  coordinates of $\sigma$, nonzero somewhere on $L$, and $B$ a nonzero
  polynomial on $K^2$. Then there are $z \in L$ and $q \in K^2$ with
  \[
    D(z) \cdot (q, 1) = 0, \qquad A(z) \neq 0, \qquad B(q) \neq 0.
  \]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:pencil-x0-fibre-intersection}
  As $\dim D(L) \ge 2$ and the constant affine functions form a line, some
  $z_1 \in L$ has $D(z_1)$ nonconstant. One of the first two coefficients
  of $D(z)$ is a linear polynomial in $z$ nonzero at $z_1$. So some
  $z_0 \in L$ has both $A(z_0) \neq 0$ and $D(z_0)$ nonconstant
  (\cref{lem:pencil-x0-fibre-intersection}). As $\dim D(L) \ge 2$, some
  $u \in L$ has $D(u)$ not a multiple of $D(z_0)$.

  For $q \in K^2$ put $w(q) = D(u)(q)\, z_0 - D(z_0)(q)\, u \in L$; then
  $D(w(q))$ vanishes at $q$. An affine function vanishing on the zero line
  of the nonconstant $D(z_0)$ is a multiple of it, so some $q_0$ on that
  line has $D(u)(q_0) \neq 0$, and there $w(q_0) = D(u)(q_0)\, z_0$. So
  $(q, s) \mapsto A(s\, w(q))$ is a polynomial, nonzero at $q_0$ and
  $s = D(u)(q_0)^{-1}$. Its product with $B$ is a nonzero polynomial, and
  at a non-root $(q, s)$ the point $z = s\, w(q)$ of $L$ has
  $D(z) \cdot (q, 1) = 0$, $A(z) \neq 0$ and $B(q) \neq 0$.
\end{proof}

We state the configuration of the one-body step for any simple graph $H$
with such an ear and any polynomial $B$, so that the step with two
interior bodies can apply it to $G_1$
(\cref{thm:pencil-x0-open-ear-two-orbit}).

\begin{lemma}[The base configuration of a one-body ear]
  \label{lem:pencil-one-ear-base}
  \lean{Graph.exists_oneEar_base}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-x0-attains, def:pencil-weighted-lifting-system,
        def:pencil-admissible-picture, def:deficiency-merged}
  Let $K$ be infinite and let $H$ be simple, with an open ear $a - x - b$
  on $V_1$ with one interior body, whose ends are not adjacent. Let
  $G' = H[V_1]$, suppose that the general configuration of $G'$ attains
  and that $\operatorname{def}_2(G'; a, b) + 2 \le
  \operatorname{def}_2(G')$ (\cref{def:deficiency-merged}, at $n = 2$),
  and let $B$ be a nonzero polynomial in the picture coordinates whose
  non-roots are admissible for $H$. Then there are a picture $q$ with
  $B(q) \neq 0$ and a polynomial $A$ in the coordinates of the lifting
  system of $H$ at $q$, nonzero at one of its solutions, such that every
  solution $(z, h)$ with $A(z, h) \neq 0$ has
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item $(h_a - h_b) \cdot (q_a, 1) \neq 0$ and
      $(h_a - h_b) \cdot (q_b, 1) \neq 0$: the point of $b$ lies off the
      plane of $a$, and the point of $a$ off the plane of $b$; and
    \item rank $6(|V_1| - 1) - \operatorname{def}(\tilde G')$ at $(q, z)$
      as a configuration of $G'$ (\cref{def:pencil-x0-attains}).
  \end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:graph-operations, lem:splitoff-deficiency-merged,
        thm:pencil-jj-equality, lem:pencil-lifting-space-deficiency,
        lem:pencil-flag-genericity, lem:pencil-one-ear-incidence,
        lem:pencil-x0-fibre-intersection, lem:pencil-three-points,
        lem:pencil-lifting-restrict, lem:pencil-picture-local,
        lem:pencil-rank-congr}
  \emph{The smaller graph.} Let $H'$ be $H$ with $x$ suppressed: the
  splitting-off $H_x^{ab}$ (\cref{def:graph-operations}) whose new edge
  $ab$ carries the label of the edge $ax$. It is $G'$ together with the
  edge $ab$, simple since $a$ and $b$ are not adjacent, and
  $\operatorname{def}_2(H') + 2 \le \operatorname{def}_2(G')$
  (\cref{lem:splitoff-deficiency-merged}, at $D = 3$).

  \emph{The incidence.} Take a picture $q'$ off the zero sets of three
  nonzero polynomials: $B$, the polynomial of the attainment at $G'$, and
  the polynomial of Jackson and Jord\'an's equality at $H'$
  (\cref{thm:pencil-jj-equality}). The equality applies to $H'$, as its
  closed neighbourhoods contain those of $G'$, which have three members at
  a picture admissible for $G'$. Then
  $\dim L_{H'}(q') = 3 + \operatorname{def}_2(H') \le \dim L_{G'}(q') - 2$
  (\cref{lem:pencil-lifting-space-deficiency}). So on the solutions of the
  lifting system of $G'$ at $q'$, the two functionals of
  \cref{lem:pencil-flag-genericity} are independent, and the linear map
  $(z, h) \mapsto h_a - h_b$ has rank at least two. Apply
  \cref{lem:pencil-one-ear-incidence} to this map. As $A$ take the product
  of the two functionals and the polynomial in the heights of the
  attainment at $G'$. Each factor is nonzero somewhere on the solutions,
  so the product is too (\cref{lem:pencil-x0-fibre-intersection}). For the
  polynomial on $K^2$ take $B$ as a polynomial in the picture point of $x$,
  with the picture fixed at $q'$ elsewhere; it is nonzero at $q'_x$. This
  gives a solution $(z', h')$ for $G'$ with $A(z', h') \neq 0$, and a
  picture point $q_x$ with $B \neq 0$ at which $h'_a - h'_b$ vanishes.

  \emph{The solution for $H$.} Let $q$ be $q'$ with $x$ moved to $q_x$.
  Then $B(q) \neq 0$, and $G'$ reads the same picture at $q$ as at $q'$
  (\cref{lem:pencil-picture-local}). Extend $(z', h')$ to $x$. The height
  at $x$ is $h'_a \cdot (q_x, 1)$, which equals $h'_b \cdot (q_x, 1)$. The
  coefficient vector at $x$ is that of the affine function through the
  three points of its closed neighbourhood $\{a, x, b\}$
  (\cref{lem:pencil-three-points}). This is a solution of the lifting
  system of $H$ at $q$, as the closed neighbourhoods of $a$ and $b$ in $H$
  are those in $G'$ with $x$ added. Read $A$ on the solutions for $H$
  through their restriction to $V_1$ (\cref{lem:pencil-lifting-restrict}).
  It is nonzero at this solution. Wherever it is nonzero, both functionals
  are nonzero, and the heights give $G'$ its target
  (\cref{lem:pencil-rank-congr}).
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[An open ear with one interior body]
  \label{thm:pencil-x0-open-ear-one}
  \lean{Graph.X0Attains.of_openEar_one}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-x0-attains, def:pencil-x0-standing, def:D-deficiency,
        def:deficiency-merged}
  Let $K$ be infinite and let $G$ satisfy the standing hypotheses
  (\cref{def:pencil-x0-standing}), with an open ear $a - x_1 - b$ on
  $V_1$ whose ends $a$ and $b$ are not adjacent. Suppose
  $\operatorname{def}(\tilde G[V_1]) \le \operatorname{def}(\tilde G)$
  (\cref{def:D-deficiency}, at $n = 3$) and
  $\operatorname{def}_2(G[V_1]; a, b) + 2 \le
  \operatorname{def}_2(G[V_1])$ (\cref{def:deficiency-merged}, at
  $n = 2$). If the general configuration of $G[V_1]$ attains, then the
  general configuration of $G$ attains.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:pencil-one-ear-base, lem:pencil-x0-main-picture-open,
        lem:pencil-ear-hinge-span, lem:block-rank-ear,
        lem:pencil-rank-congr, lem:pencil-x0-one-witness}
  \emph{The configuration.} Apply \cref{lem:pencil-one-ear-base} to $G$,
  with $B$ the main-picture polynomial of $G$
  (\cref{lem:pencil-x0-main-picture-open}). It gives a main picture $q$ of
  $G$, and at $q$ a solution of the lifting system of $G$ with
  $A \neq 0$. Its heights $z \in L_G(q)$ give $G[V_1]$ its target
  (\cref{lem:pencil-rank-congr}).

  \emph{The count.} The closed neighbourhood of $x_1$ is $\{a, x_1, b\}$,
  and its picture points are not collinear, as $q$ is admissible. So
  $p_a$, $p_{x_1}$ and $p_b$ are linearly independent, and so are the two
  joins $p_a \wedge p_{x_1}$ and $p_{x_1} \wedge p_b$. Every hinge is
  nonzero at $q$, and $\dim(\rho + \Lambda) \ge 2$
  (\cref{lem:pencil-ear-hinge-span}). By the hypothesis on the
  deficiencies this is $(\star)$ at $k = 1$, so \cref{lem:block-rank-ear}
  and \cref{lem:pencil-x0-one-witness} apply.
\end{proof}

In the step with two interior bodies, $\pi_a$ and $\pi_b$ are the planes
of $a$ and $b$ at the configuration of $G_1$, and $y$ is the point of
$x_1$ there. The points $x_1 \in \pi_a$ and $x_2 \in \pi_b$ of the next
lemma are the new points of the two interior bodies.

\begin{lemma}[Putting the second body back between the two end planes]
  \label{lem:pencil-insertion-two}
  \lean{CombinatorialRigidity.Molecular.exists_insertion_two}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:extensor, lem:pencil-join-flat}
  Let $K$ be infinite, and let $\pi_a$ and $\pi_b$ be the planes
  $p_z = \ell_a \cdot \bar p$ and $p_z = \ell_b \cdot \bar p$ of $K^4$, for
  $\ell_a, \ell_b \in K^3$ (with $\bar p$ as in \cref{lem:pencil-join-flat}).
  Let $p_a \in \pi_a$, $p_b \in \pi_b$ and $y \in \pi_a \cap \pi_b$ be
  affine points, of last coordinate $1$, with $p_a \notin \pi_b$ and
  $p_b \notin \pi_a$. Then for every subspace $\rho \subseteq \Lambda^2 K^4$
  there are affine points $x_1 \in \pi_a$ and $x_2 \in \pi_b$ with
  \[
    \dim\bigl(\rho + \operatorname{span}(p_a \wedge x_1,\ x_1 \wedge x_2,\
      x_2 \wedge p_b)\bigr)
    \;\ge\; \min\bigl(\dim(\rho + \operatorname{span}(p_a \wedge y,\
      y \wedge p_b)) + 1,\ 6\bigr).
  \]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:pencil-tetrahedron, lem:pencil-insertion}
  Write $W = \rho + \operatorname{span}(p_a \wedge y, y \wedge p_b)$. If
  $W = \Lambda^2 K^4$, take $x_1 = x_2 = y$. Otherwise note that
  $\pi_a \neq \pi_b$, as $p_b \notin \pi_a$, so the two planes meet in a
  line through $y$; let $u_0$ be a direction of it, of last coordinate
  $0$. The points $p_a$, $y$, $u_0$, $p_b$ are linearly independent: both
  plane equations vanish at $y$ and $u_0$, the equation of $\pi_a$ vanishes
  at $p_a$ but not at $p_b$ and that of $\pi_b$ the other way round, and
  $y$ is affine while $u_0$ is not. Their six joins span $\Lambda^2 K^4$
  (\cref{lem:pencil-tetrahedron}), and $W$ contains $p_a \wedge y$ and
  $y \wedge p_b$, so one of $y \wedge u_0$, $u_0 \wedge p_b$,
  $p_a \wedge u_0$, $p_a \wedge p_b$ lies outside $W$.

  Put $x_1 = y + t u$ and $x_2 = y + \sigma (p_b - y)$, with $\sigma \neq 1$
  and $u$ a direction in $\pi_a$ of last coordinate $0$. For $t \neq 0$,
  the span of $\rho$ and the three joins contains $y \wedge p_b$ (a nonzero
  multiple of $x_2 \wedge p_b$), $u \wedge x_2$, and
  $p_a \wedge y + t\,(p_a \wedge u)$.
  So if $u \wedge x_2 \notin W$, some $t \neq 0$ gives dimension at least
  $\dim W + 1$, since a vector outside a subspace stays outside it after
  adding $t$ times another for some $t \neq 0$ (as in the proof of
  \cref{lem:pencil-insertion}). It remains to choose $u$ and $\sigma$ with
  $u \wedge x_2 \notin W$. If $y \wedge u_0 \notin W$, take $u = u_0$ and
  $\sigma = 0$. Otherwise, for $\sigma \notin \{0, 1\}$, the join
  $u \wedge x_2$ is modulo $W$ a nonzero multiple of $u_0 \wedge p_b$ when
  $u = u_0$, and of $p_a \wedge u_0$ for the same curve with the two planes
  exchanged. For $u = p_a - y$ and $\sigma \notin \{0, 1\}$ it is modulo
  $W$ a nonzero multiple of $p_a \wedge p_b$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[An open ear with two interior bodies at non-adjacent ends]
  \label{thm:pencil-x0-open-ear-two-orbit}
  \lean{Graph.X0Attains.of_openEar_two_of_splitOff}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-x0-attains, def:pencil-x0-standing,
        def:graph-operations, def:deficiency-merged}
  Let $K$ be infinite and let $G$ satisfy the standing hypotheses, with
  an open ear $a - x_1 - x_2 - b$ on $V_1$ whose ends $a$ and $b$ are not
  adjacent, and suppose $\operatorname{def}_2(G[V_1]; a, b) + 2 \le
  \operatorname{def}_2(G[V_1])$ (\cref{def:deficiency-merged}, at
  $n = 2$). Let $G_1$ be $G$ with $x_2$ suppressed: the splitting-off
  $G_{x_2}^{x_1 b}$ (\cref{def:graph-operations}) whose new edge $x_1 b$
  carries the label of the deleted edge $x_1 x_2$. If the general
  configurations of $G[V_1]$ and of $G_1$ attain, then the general
  configuration of $G$ attains.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:pencil-one-ear-base, lem:pencil-x0-main-picture-open,
        lem:pencil-x0-fibre-intersection, lem:block-rank-ear,
        lem:pencil-config-point-join-rank, lem:pencil-insertion-two,
        def:pencil-ear-data, lem:pencil-ear-data, lem:pencil-ear-data-picture,
        lem:pencil-ear-data-open, lem:pencil-rank-congr,
        lem:pencil-picture-local, lem:splitoff-deficiency-reuse,
        lem:deficiency-ear, lem:pencil-x0-one-witness}
  Write $f = \operatorname{def}(\tilde G[V_1])$, and compute ranks with the
  joins as hinges (\cref{lem:pencil-config-point-join-rank}). The graph
  $G_1$ has the open ear $a - x_1 - b$ on $V_1$ and the same $G[V_1]$. It
  is simple, and its ends are not adjacent.

  \emph{The configuration of $G_1$.} Apply \cref{lem:pencil-one-ear-base}
  to $G_1$. As $B$ take the product of the polynomial of the attainment at
  $G_1$ and the main-picture polynomial of $G$
  (\cref{lem:pencil-x0-main-picture-open}). At the picture $q$ that the
  lemma gives, the non-roots of $A$ form a nonempty Zariski-open set of
  solutions of the lifting system of $G_1$. As $q$ is off the zero set of
  the polynomial of the attainment at $G_1$, the solutions whose heights
  give $G_1$ its target contain another such set. The two sets meet
  (\cref{lem:pencil-x0-fibre-intersection}). Take a solution in both, with
  heights $z$. Let $\pi_a$ and $\pi_b$ be the planes of its coefficient
  vectors at $a$ and $b$, and $y$ the point of $x_1$. Then
  $y \in \pi_a \cap \pi_b$, as $x_1$ is adjacent to $a$ and to $b$ in
  $G_1$. Also $p_b \notin \pi_a$ and $p_a \notin \pi_b$, and $G[V_1]$ and
  $G_1$ have their targets. So the ear rank formula at $G_1$
  (\cref{lem:block-rank-ear}) gives
  \[
    s \;=\; \dim\bigl(\rho + \operatorname{span}(p_a \wedge y,\ y \wedge
    p_b)\bigr) \;=\; 2 + f - \operatorname{def}(\tilde G_1).
  \]

  \emph{Putting $x_2$ back.} By \cref{lem:pencil-insertion-two} there are
  affine points $y_1 \in \pi_a$ and $y_2 \in \pi_b$ with
  $\dim(\rho + \Lambda) \ge \min(s + 1, 6)$, where
  $\Lambda = \operatorname{span}(p_a \wedge y_1,\ y_1 \wedge y_2,\
  y_2 \wedge p_b)$.

  \emph{The configuration of $G$.} Take as base data the restriction of
  $(q, z)$ to $V_1$, with the affine functions of $\pi_a$ and $\pi_b$
  (\cref{def:pencil-ear-data}). The points $y_1$ and $y_2$ at $x_1$ and
  $x_2$ are the configuration of one ear datum of $G$. So the bound
  $\dim(\rho + \Lambda) \ge \min(s + 1, 6)$ holds off the zero set of a
  polynomial in the ear data that is nonzero there
  (\cref{lem:pencil-ear-data-open}). The main-picture condition of $G$ is
  a polynomial condition on the ear data too, nonzero at the ear datum
  with picture $q$ (\cref{lem:pencil-ear-data-picture}). At a common
  non-root the picture is a main picture of $G$, so the hinges are
  nonzero, and the heights lie in $L_G$ (\cref{lem:pencil-ear-data}). The
  rank of $G[V_1]$ and $\rho$ are those of the base data
  (\cref{lem:pencil-rank-congr}, \cref{lem:pencil-picture-local}).

  \emph{The count.} $\operatorname{def}(\tilde G_1) \le
  \operatorname{def}(\tilde G)$ (\cref{lem:splitoff-deficiency-reuse}), and
  $\operatorname{def}(\tilde G) \ge f + k - 5 = f - 3$
  (\cref{lem:deficiency-ear}). So
  $\min(s + 1, 6) \ge 3 + f - \operatorname{def}(\tilde G)$, which is
  $(\star)$ at $k = 2$. By \cref{lem:block-rank-ear} and
  \cref{lem:pencil-x0-one-witness} the general configuration of $G$
  attains.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Splitting off a body of degree two}
\label{sec:main-component-splitoff}

This subsection proves the split-off step. Let $x$ be a body of degree
two whose neighbours $a$ and $b$ are not adjacent, and let
$V_1 = V(G) \setminus \{x\}$, so that $a - x - b$ is an open ear on $V_1$
(\cref{sec:main-component-chain}). Splitting off $x$ gives the graph
$G'' = G_x^{ab}$ (\cref{def:graph-operations}): it is $G[V_1]$ together
with a new edge $ab$, which carries the label of $ax$. We show that if
the general configuration of $G''$ attains, then so does that of $G$,
provided that
\[
  \delta \;=\; \operatorname{def}(\tilde G[V_1]) -
  \operatorname{def}(\tilde G[V_1]; a, b) \;\ge\; 5
\]
(\cref{def:deficiency-merged}, at $n = 3$). Here $\delta$ is how far the
deficiency of $G[V_1]$ drops when only partitions with $a$ and $b$ in one
part are counted. The structural coverage applies this step to a chain
$a - x - b$ with $\delta \ge 5$ (\cref{def:pencil-x0-usable-chain}).

The idea is to put $x$ back on the line through $a$ and $b$. Take a
configuration of $G''$ at its target rank, and place the point of $x$ on
the line through the points of $a$ and $b$, distinct from both. We call
this the \emph{special position} of $x$. There the hinges at $ax$ and
$xb$ are nonzero multiples of the hinge at $ab$, and adding $x$ raises
the rank by exactly five (\cref{lem:pencil-splitoff-special-rank}). The
hypothesis $\delta \ge 5$ is what makes five enough: it implies that the
target of $G$ is at most the target of $G''$ plus five.

The special position does not finish the proof. There $a$, $x$ and $b$
are pictured on one line, so the picture is not a main picture of $G$,
and \cref{lem:pencil-x0-one-witness} needs one. We therefore move the
picture of $x$ along a line to a general point, and move the heights
with it so that each closed neighbourhood stays in a plane
(\cref{lem:pencil-splitoff-flexes}, \cref{lem:pencil-splitoff-curve}).
Along this curve the rank falls below its starting value at only finitely
many points (\cref{lem:pencil-curve-limit}), and the picture fails to be
main at only finitely many. Any other point of the curve gives the
configuration that \cref{lem:pencil-x0-one-witness} needs.

Jackson and Jord\'an's proof of their pin-collinear
theorem~\cite{jacksonJordan2008pin} handles the same situation: a vertex
of degree two whose neighbours are not adjacent, where joining the
neighbours by an edge lowers the deficiency of the rest of the graph.
They realize the smaller graph with the new edge by induction, move the
pin of that edge slightly, and then add back the vertex by a 1-extension
and a 0-extension. We reverse the order: we put $x$ back first, at the
special position, where the rank is known exactly, and then move it.
Since we move along a polynomial curve rather than within a small
Euclidean neighbourhood, the argument works over every infinite field.

We begin with the lower semicontinuity of the rank
(\cref{lem:rank-polynomial-of-le-finrank}) along a polynomial curve of
normals.

\begin{lemma}[The rank along a polynomial curve of normals]
  \label{lem:pencil-curve-limit}
  \lean{CombinatorialRigidity.Molecular.PanelHingeFramework.finite_setOf_finrank_lt_of_curve}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:panel-hinge-framework, def:panel-support-extensor,
        def:rigidity-matrix}
  Let $G$ be a finite graph, and let $t \mapsto n(t) = (n_v(t))$ be panel
  normals whose coordinates are polynomials in $t \in K$, such that the
  hinge at every edge of $G$ is nonzero at $t = 0$. Then for all but
  finitely many $t \in K$, the rank of the rigidity matrix of the
  panel-hinge framework on $G$ with normals $n(t)$ is at least its value
  at $t = 0$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:rank-polynomial-of-le-finrank}
  By \cref{lem:rank-polynomial-of-le-finrank} at $n(0)$ there is a
  polynomial $Q$ in the normal coordinates, nonzero at $n(0)$, such that
  the rank is at least its value at $n(0)$ wherever $Q$ is nonzero. The
  polynomial $t \mapsto Q(n(t))$ in one variable is nonzero at $t = 0$, so
  it has finitely many roots, and off them the bound holds.
\end{proof}

The next lemma computes the rank at the special position. It is stated
for panel normals in $K^{k+2}$; configurations are the case $k = 2$,
where $D - 1 = 5$.

\begin{lemma}[The rank at the special position]
  \label{lem:pencil-splitoff-special-rank}
  \lean{Graph.finrank_span_rigidityRows_splitOff_special,
    CombinatorialRigidity.Molecular.BodyHingeFramework.finrank_span_rigidityRows_eq_add_of_motions}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:graph-operations, def:panel-hinge-framework,
        def:rigidity-matrix}
  Let $G$ have an open ear $a - x - b$ on $V_1$ with one interior body,
  and let $G''$ be the splitting-off $G_x^{ab}$
  (\cref{def:graph-operations}) whose new edge $ab$ carries the label of
  the edge $ax$. Let $(n_v)$ be panel normals in $K^{k+2}$ with $n_a$ and
  $n_b$ linearly independent and
  \[
    n_x = (1 - s)\, n_a + s\, n_b, \qquad s \neq 0, 1.
  \]
  Then the rank of the rigidity matrix of the panel-hinge framework on
  $G$ with these normals is that of $G''$ plus $D - 1$, where
  $D = \binom{k+2}{2}$ is the dimension of the screw space.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:panel-support-extensor, def:hinge-constraint,
        lem:span-rigidityRows-eq-of-motions-eq}
  The panel support extensor is bilinear and alternating
  (\cref{def:panel-support-extensor}), so up to sign the hinges at $ax$
  and $xb$ are $s\,C$ and $(1 - s)\,C$, where $C \neq 0$ is the hinge at
  $ab$ in $G''$; every other edge has the same hinge in $G$ and in $G''$.
  Since $S_x - S_b = (S_x - S_a) + (S_a - S_b)$, a screw assignment $S$ is
  therefore a motion of $G$ if and only if it is a motion of $G''$ with
  $S_x - S_a \in K C$. As $x$ is not a body of $G''$, the map
  $S \mapsto S_x - S_a$ takes the motions of $G''$ onto the screw space,
  so the motions of $G$ have codimension $D - 1$ in those of $G''$. The
  row spaces are the annihilators of the motion spaces
  (\cref{lem:span-rigidityRows-eq-of-motions-eq}), so the ranks differ by
  $D - 1$.
\end{proof}

To choose the special position we need one more lemma. A solution of
the lifting system is a pair $(z, h)$: heights $z$, and for each body
$v$ an affine function $h_v$ that agrees with $z$ on the closed
neighbourhood of $v$ (\cref{def:pencil-weighted-lifting-system}). The
curve needs a special point at which $h_a - h_b$ is nonzero on some
solution for $G[V_1]$, unless $h_a = h_b$ on every solution
(\cref{lem:pencil-splitoff-curve}(1)). Such a point exists unless every
solution has $h_a - h_b$ vanishing on the line through $q_a$ and $q_b$.
The next lemma shows that in that case $h_a = h_b$ on every solution. It
is stated for pairs $(z, h)$, not for heights alone, because at a body of
degree one in $G[V_1]$ the heights do not determine $h_v$.

\begin{lemma}[End planes that all meet over the line of the ends coincide]
  \label{lem:pencil-splitoff-flexes}
  \lean{Graph.planeDiff_eq_zero_of_splitOff}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:graph-operations, def:pencil-weighted-lifting-system,
        def:pencil-lifting-space, def:pencil-admissible-picture}
  Let $G$ have an open ear $a - x - b$ on $V_1$ with one interior body,
  let $G' = G[V_1]$, and let $G''$ be the splitting-off $G_x^{ab}$ whose
  new edge $ab$ carries the label of the edge $ax$. Let $q$ be admissible
  for $G$ and for $G''$, with $\dim L_{G''}(q) \le \dim L_G(q)$. If every
  solution $(z, h)$ of the lifting system of $G'$ at $q$
  (\cref{def:pencil-weighted-lifting-system}) has
  \[
    (h_a - h_b) \cdot (q_a, 1) = 0 \qquad\text{and}\qquad
    (h_a - h_b) \cdot (q_b, 1) = 0,
  \]
  then every such solution has $h_a = h_b$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:pencil-x0-one-witness, lem:pencil-three-points}
  Suppose some solution $y_0$ has $h_a \neq h_b$. The affine function
  $h_a - h_b$ vanishes at $q_a$ and $q_b$, and $q_a$, $q_b$, $q_x$ are
  not collinear, as $q$ is admissible for $G$. So the functional
  $\varphi(z, h) = (h_a - h_b) \cdot (q_x, 1)$ is nonzero at $y_0$.

  We compare the solution spaces of the three lifting systems at $q$.
  First, every solution for $G'$ is one for $G''$. Indeed, the closed
  neighbourhoods of $a$ and $b$ in $G''$ gain $b$ and $a$ respectively,
  and by hypothesis $h_a \cdot (q_b, 1) = h_b \cdot (q_b, 1) = z_b$ and
  $h_b \cdot (q_a, 1) = z_a$. Second, restriction to $V_1$ maps the
  solutions for $G$ injectively into the solutions for $G'$ on which
  $\varphi$ vanishes. A restricted solution is a solution for $G'$, since
  each closed neighbourhood in $G'$ lies inside the one in $G$. And
  $\varphi$ vanishes on it, since $x$ lies in the closed neighbourhoods of
  both $a$ and $b$ in $G$, so $h_a$ and $h_b$ both take the value $z_x$
  at $q_x$. The map is injective: a solution for $G$ that vanishes on
  $V_1$ has $z_x = h_a \cdot (q_x, 1) = 0$, so $h_x$ vanishes at the three
  non-collinear points $q_a, q_x, q_b$, and $h_x = 0$ (as in
  \cref{lem:pencil-three-points}). Since $\varphi(y_0) \neq 0$, the
  solutions for $G$ form a space of smaller dimension than the solutions
  for $G'$, and hence than those for $G''$. At a picture admissible for a
  graph, the solutions form a space of dimension $\dim L(q)$ (the proof of
  \cref{lem:pencil-x0-one-witness}). So $\dim L_G(q) < \dim L_{G''}(q)$, a
  contradiction.
\end{proof}

The last lemma collects three facts used to build the curve.

\begin{lemma}[A line of solutions through the special position]
  \label{lem:pencil-splitoff-curve}
  \lean{CombinatorialRigidity.Molecular.exists_mem_forall_add_smul_eq_zero,
    Graph.mem_liftingSpace_oneEar, Graph.ker_liftingMatrix_congr}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-weighted-lifting-system, def:pencil-lifting-space,
        def:pencil-admissible-picture}
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item Let $L$ be a subspace of a vector space over $K$, let
      $\varphi_0$ and $\psi$ be linear functionals, and let $y_0 \in L$
      with $\varphi_0(y_0) = 0$. Suppose that $\varphi_0$ is nonzero
      somewhere on $L$, or that $\varphi_0$ and $\psi$ both vanish on $L$.
      Then some $w \in L$ has
      $\varphi_0(y_0 + t w) + t\, \psi(y_0 + t w) = 0$ for every
      $t \in K$.
    \item Let $G$ have an open ear $a - x - b$ on $V_1$ with one interior
      body, and let $q$ be admissible for $G$. If a solution $(z, h)$ of
      the lifting system of $G[V_1]$ at $q$ has
      $(h_a - h_b) \cdot (q_x, 1) = 0$, then the height that agrees with
      $z$ away from $x$ and takes the value $h_a \cdot (q_x, 1)$ at $x$
      lies in $L_G(q)$.
    \item The solutions of the lifting system of a graph $H$ at a picture
      depend only on the picture at the bodies of $H$.
  \end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:pencil-three-points, lem:pencil-picture-local}
  (1) If $\varphi_0(W) \neq 0$ for some $W \in L$, take
  $w = \varphi_0(W)^{-1}\bigl(\psi(W)\, y_0 - \psi(y_0)\, W\bigr)$. Then
  $\varphi_0(y_0 + t w) = -t\,\psi(y_0)$ and
  $\psi(y_0 + t w) = \psi(y_0)$. (One finds this $w$ by solving the
  condition on the line $y_0 + K W$ at each $t$. The solution is rational
  in $t$, and as the condition is homogeneous it can be rescaled to clear
  the denominator.) Otherwise $\varphi_0$ and $\psi$ vanish on $L$, and
  $w = 0$ will do.

  (2) At a body $v$ of $V_1$, the closed neighbourhood in $G$ is the one
  in $G[V_1]$, with $x$ added when $v$ is $a$ or $b$. The height agrees
  with $h_v$ on it: at $x$, because $h_a$ and $h_b$ agree at
  $(q_x, 1)$. The closed neighbourhood of $x$ is $\{a, x, b\}$, and every
  height agrees with an affine function on three points
  (\cref{lem:pencil-three-points}).

  (3) Each condition of the lifting system of $H$ reads the picture at a
  member of the closed neighbourhood of a body of $H$, and these are
  bodies of $H$ (as in \cref{lem:pencil-picture-local}).
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Splitting off a body of degree two]
  \label{thm:pencil-x0-splitoff}
  \lean{Graph.X0Attains.of_splitOff}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-x0-attains, def:pencil-x0-standing,
        def:graph-operations, def:D-deficiency, def:deficiency-merged}
  Let $K$ be infinite and let $G$ satisfy the standing hypotheses
  (\cref{def:pencil-x0-standing}), with an open ear $a - x - b$ on $V_1$
  with one interior body whose ends $a$ and $b$ are not adjacent. Let
  $G''$ be $G$ with $x$ suppressed: the splitting-off $G_x^{ab}$
  (\cref{def:graph-operations}) whose new edge $ab$ carries the label of
  the edge $ax$. Suppose $\operatorname{def}(\tilde G[V_1]; a, b) + 5 \le
  \operatorname{def}(\tilde G[V_1])$ (\cref{def:deficiency-merged},
  \cref{def:D-deficiency}, at $n = 3$). If the general configuration of
  $G''$ attains, then the general configuration of $G$ attains.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:splitoff-deficiency-merged, lem:deficiency-ear,
        lem:splitoff-deficiency-reuse, thm:pencil-jj-equality,
        lem:pencil-lifting-space-deficiency,
        lem:pencil-x0-main-picture-open, lem:pencil-x0-one-witness,
        lem:pencil-rank-congr, lem:pencil-curve-limit,
        lem:pencil-splitoff-special-rank, lem:pencil-splitoff-flexes,
        lem:pencil-splitoff-curve}
  \emph{The counts.} The new edge $ab$ lowers by five the value of every
  partition of $V_1$ that separates $a$ and $b$, and by hypothesis the
  other partitions are already five below
  $\operatorname{def}(\tilde G[V_1])$. So
  $\operatorname{def}(\tilde G'') + 5 \le
  \operatorname{def}(\tilde G[V_1])$
  (\cref{lem:splitoff-deficiency-merged}, at $D = 6$). With
  $\operatorname{def}(\tilde G[V_1]) \le \operatorname{def}(\tilde G) + 4$
  (\cref{lem:deficiency-ear}, at $k = 1$) this gives
  $\operatorname{def}(\tilde G'') + 1 \le \operatorname{def}(\tilde G)$.
  Also $\operatorname{def}_2(G'') \le \operatorname{def}_2(G)$
  (\cref{lem:splitoff-deficiency-reuse}, at $n = 2$).

  \emph{The picture.} As $a$ and $b$ are not adjacent, $G''$ is simple
  and each of its closed neighbourhoods has as many members as in $G$, at
  least three. So Jackson and Jord\'an's equality applies to $G''$
  (\cref{thm:pencil-jj-equality}). Take a picture $q$ off the zero sets
  of three nonzero polynomials: that of the attainment at
  $G''$, that of the equality at $G''$, and the main-picture polynomial
  of $G$ (\cref{lem:pencil-x0-main-picture-open}). Then $q$ is admissible
  for $G''$ and a main picture of $G$, some $z'' \in L_{G''}(q)$ gives
  $G''$ its target rank, and
  \[
    \dim L_{G''}(q) \;=\; 3 + \operatorname{def}_2(G'') \;\le\;
    3 + \operatorname{def}_2(G) \;\le\; \dim L_G(q)
  \]
  (\cref{lem:pencil-lifting-space-deficiency}). Let $y_0 = (z'', h)$ be
  a solution of the lifting system of $G''$ at $q$ with heights $z''$.
  It is a solution for $G[V_1]$, and $h_a - h_b$ vanishes at $(q_a, 1)$
  and $(q_b, 1)$, since $ab$ is an edge of $G''$.

  \emph{The special position.} If some solution for $G[V_1]$ has
  $h_a - h_b$ nonzero at $(q_a, 1)$ or at $(q_b, 1)$, then for all but
  at most one $s$ it is nonzero at $(1 - s)(q_a, 1) + s\,(q_b, 1)$.
  Otherwise every solution for $G[V_1]$ has $h_a = h_b$
  (\cref{lem:pencil-splitoff-flexes}). Fix $s \neq 0, 1$ accordingly, put
  $q^0_x = (1 - s) q_a + s\, q_b$, and let
  $\varphi_0(z, h) = (h_a - h_b) \cdot (q^0_x, 1)$ and
  $\psi(z, h) = (h_a - h_b) \cdot (q_x - q^0_x, 0)$. By
  \cref{lem:pencil-splitoff-curve}(1) there is a solution $w$ for
  $G[V_1]$ such that $y(t) = y_0 + t w$ satisfies
  $\varphi_0(y(t)) + t\,\psi(y(t)) = 0$ for every $t$.

  \emph{The curve.} Let $q(t)$ be $q$ with the picture of $x$ moved to
  $q^0_x + t\,(q_x - q^0_x)$, so that $q(1) = q$. As $q(t)$ agrees with
  $q$ on $V_1$, $y(t)$ is a solution for $G[V_1]$ at $q(t)$
  (\cref{lem:pencil-splitoff-curve}(3)), and the identity above says
  that its $h_a - h_b$ vanishes at $(q(t)_x, 1)$. Let $z(t)$ be the
  height of $y(t)$ on $V_1$, extended by $h_a \cdot (q(t)_x, 1)$ at $x$.
  The points of the configuration $(q(t), z(t))$ are polynomials in $t$.
  At $t = 0$ they are at the special position:
  $p_x = (1 - s) p_a + s\, p_b$, because $h_a$ takes the values $z''_a$
  and $z''_b$ at $q_a$ and $q_b$. Also $p_a$ and $p_b$ are independent, as
  $q_a \neq q_b$. Every hinge of $G$ is nonzero at $t = 0$: $q^0_x$
  differs from $q_a$ and $q_b$ as $s \neq 0, 1$, and every other edge is
  an edge of $G''$. So at $t = 0$ the rank of $G$ is that of $G''$ at
  $(q, z'')$ plus five
  (\cref{lem:pencil-splitoff-special-rank}, \cref{lem:pencil-rank-congr}).

  \emph{A good parameter.} By \cref{lem:pencil-curve-limit}, the rank of
  $G$ at $(q(t), z(t))$ is at least its value at $t = 0$ for all but
  finitely many $t$. The main-picture polynomial of $G$ at $q(t)$ is a
  polynomial in $t$, nonzero at $t = 1$, so $q(t)$ is a main picture of
  $G$ for all but finitely many $t$. Take $t$ outside both finite sets.
  Then $z(t) \in L_G(q(t))$ (\cref{lem:pencil-splitoff-curve}(2)), and
  $(q(t), z(t))$ has rank at least
  \[
    6(|V_1| - 1) - \operatorname{def}(\tilde G'') + 5 \;\ge\;
    6(|V(G)| - 1) - \operatorname{def}(\tilde G),
  \]
  as $|V(G)| = |V_1| + 1$. By \cref{lem:pencil-x0-one-witness} the
  general configuration of $G$ attains.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Contraction at an additive core}
\label{sec:main-component-contract-additive}

This subsection proves the contraction step at a rigid core whose planar
deficiency need not vanish. Let $W \subsetneq V(G)$, $r \in W$, the core
$H = G[W]$ and the contraction $G/H$ be as in
\cref{sec:main-component-contract}. Suppose that
$\operatorname{def}(\tilde H) = 0$, and that the planar deficiencies of
$H$ and $G/H$ add up to at most that of $G$:
\[
  \operatorname{def}_2(H) + \operatorname{def}_2(G/H) \;\le\;
  \operatorname{def}_2(G).
\]
We show that if the general configurations of both $H$ and $G/H$ attain,
then so does that of $G$. The step is again Katoh and Tanigawa's
contraction case~\cite[\S6.2, Lemma~6.3]{katohTanigawa2011}, read on
configurations. Their proof, too, takes realizations of both the rigid
subgraph and the contracted graph from the
induction~\cite[p.~674]{katohTanigawa2011}. The structural coverage
applies the step at the core that \cref{thm:pencil-x0-theorem-s}
provides.

We follow the argument of \cref{sec:main-component-contract}, which fits
the scheme of \cref{sec:main-component-cut}. The picture and the count
are as there. The height is again found along the contraction curve
$q(t)$, with the rescaled lifting system $M(t)$
(\cref{def:pencil-contract-lifting-system}). A solution of $M(0)$
extends to solutions of $M(t)$ at all but finitely many $t$, by the
polynomial section of the proof of \cref{lem:pencil-x0-one-witness},
provided that the kernel does not jump at $t = 0$. At a core of planar
deficiency zero, over a general picture, every solution of $M(t)$ puts
the core on one plane. That plane bounds $\ker M(0)$ above, and it gives
the rank of the core along the curve. At an additive core the core need
not lie on one plane, so we argue both points differently.

Under additivity the two bounds on $\ker M(0)$ meet. Above, the kernel is
bounded through the core heights of its solutions, which lie in $L_H(q)$
(\cref{lem:pencil-contract-kernel-bound}). Below, it is at least as large
as the lifting space of $G$ at a general point of the curve, whose
dimension is at least $3 + \operatorname{def}_2(G)$. With Jackson and
Jord\'an's equality at $H$ and at $G/H$, and additivity, the upper bound
is at most $3 + \operatorname{def}_2(G)$ too. So the kernel does not
jump. As the bounds meet, the core heights of the solutions of $M(0)$
are all of $L_H(q)$.

We need all of $L_H(q)$ for a new open condition on $\ker M(0)$: that the
core heights of the solution give $H$ its target over $q$. It holds
somewhere on $\ker M(0)$, as the heights in $L_H(q)$ at which $H$ has its
target contain a nonempty Zariski-open subset. The other condition is
the one of \cref{lem:pencil-contract-degenerate-rank}, as at a core of
planar deficiency zero: at the end of the curve, the rows of the edges
outside the core, with the columns of $W$ deleted, have rank at least
$\operatorname{tgt}(G/H)$. So two open conditions meet in one linear
space, as in the scheme, but in $\ker M(0)$ rather than in $L_G(q)$
(\cref{lem:pencil-x0-fibre-intersection}).

Last, we read the rank of the core along the curve over the fixed
picture $q$. At $t \neq 0$ the configuration of $H$ along the curve is
the image of its configuration over $q$, with the core heights of the
solution, under an invertible linear map of $K^4$
(\cref{lem:pencil-contract-magnified-rank}). Along the polynomial
section through a solution that satisfies both conditions, the new
condition holds at all but finitely many $t$. There the core has its
target $6(|W| - 1)$.

\begin{lemma}[The rank of the core along the curve]
  \label{lem:pencil-contract-magnified-rank}
  \lean{Graph.finrank_span_rigidityRows_induce_contractHeight_eq}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-contract-lifting-system, def:pencil-x0-attains}
  Let $r \in W$ and $H = G[W]$, let $q$ be a picture and $t \in K$
  nonzero, and let $(z, h)$ be any vector of unknowns of the rescaled
  lifting system. Then the rank of $H$ at the configuration
  $(q(t), z(t))$ equals its rank at $(q, z)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:pencil-rank-collineation}
  At a body $c$ of $W$ the point of $(q(t), z(t))$ is
  $((1 - t)\,x_r + t\,x_c,\ (1 - t)\,y_r + t\,y_c,\ t\,z_c,\ 1)$, the
  image of $(x_c, y_c, z_c, 1)$ under the linear map
  \[
    (\xi, \eta, \zeta, \omega) \mapsto
    (t\,\xi + (1 - t)\,x_r\,\omega,\ t\,\eta + (1 - t)\,y_r\,\omega,\
     t\,\zeta,\ \omega)
  \]
  of $K^4$, which is invertible as $t \neq 0$. So
  \cref{lem:pencil-rank-collineation} applies.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Contraction at an additive core]
  \label{thm:pencil-x0-contract-additive}
  \lean{Graph.X0Attains.of_additiveContract}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-x0-attains, def:pencil-x0-standing, def:cut-edges-2ec,
        def:rigid-contraction, def:D-deficiency}
  Let $K$ be infinite and let $G$ satisfy the standing hypotheses and be
  $2$-edge-connected. Let $W \subsetneq V(G)$ with $|W| \ge 2$ and
  $\operatorname{def}(\tilde G[W]) = 0$, such that no body outside $W$ is
  adjacent to two bodies of $W$, and let $G/H$ be the contraction of
  $H = G[W]$ to a body of $W$. Suppose
  $\operatorname{def}_2(H) + \operatorname{def}_2(G/H) \le
  \operatorname{def}_2(G)$. If the general configurations of $H$ and of
  $G/H$ attain, then the general configuration of $G$ attains.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:pencil-contract-standing-rigid, lem:pencil-contract-standing,
        lem:pencil-contraction-deficiency, thm:pencil-jj-equality,
        lem:pencil-x0-main-picture-open, lem:pencil-rank-scale-shift,
        lem:pencil-x0-one-witness, lem:pencil-lifting-space-deficiency,
        lem:pencil-contract-lifting-kernel,
        lem:pencil-contract-kernel-bound, lem:pencil-contract-limit,
        lem:pencil-contract-degenerate-rank,
        lem:pencil-x0-fibre-intersection,
        lem:pencil-contract-magnified-rank, lem:pencil-rank-congr,
        lem:block-rank-contract}
  \emph{The pieces.} $H$ satisfies the standing hypotheses
  (\cref{lem:pencil-contract-standing-rigid}, at $n = 3$). By
  \cref{lem:pencil-contract-standing}, $G/H$ is simple and satisfies them
  too. As $H$ is rigid, $G/H$ has the deficiency of $G$
  (\cref{lem:pencil-contraction-deficiency}, at $n = 3$).

  \emph{The picture.} As $H$ and $G/H$ satisfy the standing hypotheses,
  Jackson and Jord\'an's equality applies to both
  (\cref{thm:pencil-jj-equality}). Take a picture $q$ off
  the zero sets of five nonzero polynomials: those of the attainment and
  of the equality at $H$ and at $G/H$, and the main-picture polynomial of
  $G$ (\cref{lem:pencil-x0-main-picture-open}). Then $q$ is admissible for
  $H$ and for $G/H$, and both have attaining heights over $q$. Also
  $\dim L_H(q) = 3 + \operatorname{def}_2(H)$ and
  $\dim L_{G/H}(q) = 3 + \operatorname{def}_2(G/H)$. Subtracting the
  constant height at $r$ does not change the rank of $G/H$
  (\cref{lem:pencil-rank-scale-shift}). So $G/H$ has an attaining height
  $z'$ with $z'_r = 0$.

  \emph{The kernel at the end of the curve.} Let $\rho$ take a vector of
  unknowns to its core heights, as in
  \cref{lem:pencil-contract-kernel-bound}. The polynomial section through
  the zero solution at $t = 0$ (the proof of
  \cref{lem:pencil-x0-one-witness}, with the parameter $t$) gives
  $\dim \ker M(t) \le \dim \ker M(0)$ for all but finitely many $t$. For
  all but finitely many $t$, also, $q(t)$ is a main picture of $G$, as
  the main-picture polynomial of $G$ along the curve is nonzero at
  $t = 1$, where $q(1) = q$. At a $t \neq 0$ with both properties,
  \[
    3 + \operatorname{def}_2(G) \le \dim L_G(q(t)) \le \dim \ker M(t)
    \le \dim \ker M(0)
  \]
  (\cref{lem:pencil-lifting-space-deficiency},
  \cref{lem:pencil-contract-lifting-kernel}). By
  \cref{lem:pencil-contract-kernel-bound}, Jackson and Jord\'an's
  equality at $H$ and $G/H$, and additivity,
  \[
    \dim \ker M(0) + 3 \le \dim \rho(\ker M(0)) + \dim L_{G/H}(q)
    \le \dim L_H(q) + \dim L_{G/H}(q) \le 6 + \operatorname{def}_2(G).
  \]
  So $\dim \ker M(0) = 3 + \operatorname{def}_2(G)$, and the second chain
  is an equality. Hence $\rho(\ker M(0))$, which lies in $L_H(q)$, has the
  dimension of $L_H(q)$: the core heights of the solutions of $M(0)$ are
  all of $L_H(q)$.

  \emph{Two open conditions.} The polynomial of the attainment at $H$
  over $q$ is nonzero somewhere on $L_H(q)$. Composed with $\rho$, it is
  therefore nonzero somewhere on $\ker M(0)$. Let $Q$ be the polynomial of
  \cref{lem:pencil-contract-degenerate-rank} for the rank
  $\operatorname{tgt}(G/H)$ of $G/H$ at $(q, z')$, and read it at the
  configuration $(q(0), z(0))$ of a solution. It is nonzero at the
  solution that agrees with $z'$ off $W$
  (\cref{lem:pencil-contract-limit}), as the configuration of that
  solution at $t = 0$ is the collapsed placement of $(q, z')$. So some
  $x_0 \in \ker M(0)$ makes both polynomials nonzero
  (\cref{lem:pencil-x0-fibre-intersection}).

  \emph{The curve.} Apply the polynomial section of the proof of
  \cref{lem:pencil-x0-one-witness} to $M(t)$, at $t = 0$ and $x_0$. It
  gives a polynomial $D$ in $t$ with $D(0) \neq 0$, and a vector $X(t)$
  of polynomials in $t$ with $X(0) = x_0$. At every $t \neq 0$ with
  $q(t)$ a main picture of $G$ the kernel does not jump:
  $\dim \ker M(0) = 3 + \operatorname{def}_2(G) \le \dim L_G(q(t)) \le
  \dim \ker M(t)$. So $X(t)$ solves $M(t)$ at every such $t$ with
  $D(t) \neq 0$, and its heights $z(t)$ lie in $L_G(q(t))$
  (\cref{lem:pencil-contract-lifting-kernel}).

  \emph{A good parameter.} Take $t$ off the zero sets of $t$, of $D$, of
  the main-picture polynomial of $G$ along the curve, and of the two
  polynomials of the open conditions along $X(t)$, which are nonzero at
  $t = 0$. Then $\rho(X(t))$ lies in $L_H(q)$
  (\cref{lem:pencil-contract-kernel-bound}(1)), and it is an attaining
  height of $H$ over $q$. So $H$ has rank $6(|W| - 1)$ at
  $(q, \rho(X(t)))$, and hence at $(q(t), z(t))$
  (\cref{lem:pencil-contract-magnified-rank}, \cref{lem:pencil-rank-congr}).
  As $Q$ is nonzero at $(q(t), z(t))$, the rows of the other edges there,
  with the columns of $W$ deleted, have rank at least
  $\operatorname{tgt}(G/H)$. By \cref{lem:block-rank-contract} the rank of
  $G$ at $(q(t), z(t))$ is at least
  $6(|W| - 1) + \operatorname{tgt}(G/H) = \operatorname{tgt}(G)$, since
  $|V(G/H)| = |V(G)| - |W| + 1$ and $G/H$ has the deficiency of $G$. So
  \cref{lem:pencil-x0-one-witness} applies.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The deficiency of a sparse graph}
\label{sec:main-component-sparse}

This subsection proves the estimates on partitions that the structural
coverage uses (\cref{sec:main-component-coverage}). The proof of the
generic statement uses some of them too
(\cref{sec:main-component-statements}). Let $D$ be the dimension of
\cref{def:D-deficiency} at some $n$. We call the $D$-deficiency
$\operatorname{def}_{\tilde G}(P)$ of a partition $P$ its \emph{value}.
For $X \subseteq V(G)$ let $e(X)$ be the number of edges of $G$ joining
two distinct bodies of $X$, and let
\[
  s_D(X) \;=\; D\,(|X| - 1) \;-\; (D - 1)\,e(X),
\]
the value of the partition of $G[X]$ into singletons. We call $s_D(X)$
the \emph{singleton value} of $X$, and write $s = s_3$, the case
$n = 2$. A set $W$ of at least two bodies is \emph{rigid} if $G[W]$ is a
rigid subgraph at $n = 3$ (\cref{def:rigid-subgraph}), that is, if
$\operatorname{def}_3(G[W]) = 0$. It is \emph{planar-rigid} if
$\operatorname{def}_2(G[W]) = 0$. For bodies $a$ and $b$,
$\operatorname{def}_n(G; a, b)$ is the merged deficiency of
\cref{def:deficiency-merged}.

The graph $G$ is \emph{sparse} if $s(X) \ge 1$ for every set $X$ of at
least two bodies, that is, if every such set spans at most
$\frac12(3|X| - 4)$ edges. Sparsity is the absence of planar-rigid sets.
Indeed, $\operatorname{def}_2(G[X])$ is at least the value $s(X)$ of the
singletons, which is positive in a sparse graph. Conversely, a graph with
no planar-rigid set is sparse (\cref{lem:deficiency-sparse}). For a graph
without loops, sparsity says that the multigraph $2G$, with every edge of
$G$ doubled, is $(3, 4)$-sparse (\cref{def:graph-sparse}). A set $X$ of
at least three bodies is \emph{tight} if $G[X]$ is sparse and
$s(X) = 1$, so that $X$ spans as many edges as sparsity allows. For
example, a set inducing a $4$-cycle is tight.

In a sparse graph, a partition with a part $Z$ has value at most
$s(V(G)) - s(Z)$ at $n = 2$ (\cref{lem:deficiency-singleton-bound}). As
$s(Z) \ge 0$, the partition into singletons attains the planar
deficiency: $\operatorname{def}_2(G) = s(V(G))$. The estimates below
compare singleton values in this way. We use three further facts. Adding
a body with $t$ edges into a set changes its singleton value by
$D - (D - 1)t$ (\cref{lem:deficiency-add-body}). Merging the parts of a
partition that meet a set of deficiency zero does not lower its value
(\cref{lem:deficiency-merge-rigid}). The third relates the two
dimensions. Let a partition $P$ have value $v_2(P)$ at $n = 2$ and
$v_3(P)$ at $n = 3$, and let $d(P)$ edges of $G$ join distinct parts of
$P$. Then
\[
  v_3(P) \;=\; 6\,(|P| - 1) - 5\,d(P) \;=\; 2\,v_2(P) - d(P).
\]
So a partition whose value at $n = 2$ is at most zero has value at most
zero at $n = 3$. Planar counts can therefore show that a set is rigid.

From these facts we prove that a tight set is rigid, and that in a
sparse graph a maximal rigid set has the least singleton value among the
sets containing it. At a set $W$ of least singleton value among the sets
containing it, the planar deficiencies of $G[W]$ and of $G/G[W]$ add up
to at most that of $G$. The contraction step at an additive core
assumes this inequality (\cref{sec:main-component-contract-additive}).
Last, we bound the merged deficiencies at the ends of an open ear with
one or two interior bodies. The split-off step assumes such a bound for
one interior body (\cref{sec:main-component-splitoff}). So do the ear
steps at non-adjacent ends, for one or two
(\cref{sec:main-component-orbit}).

\begin{lemma}[The singletons bound the partitions of a sparse graph]
  \label{lem:deficiency-singleton-bound}
  \lean{Graph.partitionDef_add_partitionDef_induce_id_le, Graph.partitionDef_le_partitionDef_id}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:D-deficiency}
  Let $P$ be a partition of $V(G)$ with a part $Z$, and suppose
  $s_D(Y) \ge 0$ for every nonempty $Y \subseteq V(G) \setminus Z$.
  Then
  \[
    \operatorname{def}_{\tilde G}(P) + s_D(Z) \;\le\; s_D(V(G)).
  \]
  In particular, if $s_D(Y) \ge 0$ for every nonempty $Y \subseteq
  V(G)$, then $\operatorname{def}_{\tilde G}(P) \le s_D(V(G))$ for every
  partition $P$ of $V(G)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:cut-edge-decomposition}
  Both $P$ and the partition into singletons are separated by $Z$, so
  each splits at $Z$ with the same cut term
  (\cref{lem:cut-edge-decomposition}). On $G[Z]$ the partition $P$ has
  one part and value zero, and the singletons have value $s_D(Z)$. The
  second statement follows by induction on the number of bodies, since
  $s_D(Z) \ge 0$ and the rest is the statement at $G[V(G) \setminus
  Z]$. The first follows from one such split and the second statement
  at $G[V(G) \setminus Z]$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Adding one body]
  \label{lem:deficiency-add-body}
  \lean{Graph.partitionDef_induce_insert, Graph.deficiency_induce_insert_eq_zero,
        Graph.partitionDef_two_induce_insert_id}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:D-deficiency}
  Let $X \subseteq V(G)$ and $u \notin X$, and let $P$ be a partition of
  $X \cup \{u\}$. Let $t$ be the number of edges of $G$ from $u$ to a
  body of $X$ in a part of $P$ other than the part of $u$, and let
  $\epsilon = 1$ if $u$ is alone in its part and $\epsilon = 0$
  otherwise. Then
  \[
    \operatorname{def}_{\tilde G[X \cup \{u\}]}(P) \;=\;
    \operatorname{def}_{\tilde G[X]}(P|_X) + \epsilon D - (D - 1)\,t.
  \]
  Consequently, if $D \ge 3$, $\operatorname{def}(\tilde G[W]) = 0$ and
  a body $u \notin W$ is adjacent to two distinct bodies of $W$, then
  $\operatorname{def}(\tilde G[W \cup \{u\}]) = 0$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  The number of parts grows by one exactly when $u$ is alone, and the
  crossing edges gain the $t$ edges at $u$. For the consequence, let $P$
  partition $W \cup \{u\}$. If $u$ shares a part, the value of $P$ is at
  most that of $P|_W$, which is at most zero. If $u$ is alone, both of
  its edges into $W$ cross, and the value is at most
  $0 + D - 2(D - 1) < 0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[A set of nonpositive singleton value contains a set of deficiency zero]
  \label{lem:deficiency-sparse}
  \lean{Graph.exists_deficiency_induce_eq_zero_of_partitionDef_id_nonpos,
        Graph.one_le_partitionDef_induce_id}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:D-deficiency}
  Let $X \subseteq V(G)$ with $|X| \ge 2$ and $s_D(X) \le 0$. Then some
  $Y \subseteq X$ with $|Y| \ge 2$ has $\operatorname{def}(\tilde G[Y])
  = 0$. Hence, if no set of at least two bodies has deficiency zero,
  then $s_D(X) \ge 1$ for every $X$ with $|X| \ge 2$; at $n = 2$, a
  graph with no planar-rigid set is sparse.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:deficiency-singleton-bound}
  Take $Y \subseteq X$ minimal with $|Y| \ge 2$ and $s_D(Y) \le 0$.
  Every nonempty proper subset of $Y$ then has nonnegative singleton
  value, a singleton having value zero. A partition of $Y$ other than
  $\{Y\}$ has a part $Z \subsetneq Y$, and its value is at most $s_D(Y)
  - s_D(Z) \le 0$ (\cref{lem:deficiency-singleton-bound}); the partition
  $\{Y\}$ has value zero.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Merging along a set of deficiency zero]
  \label{lem:deficiency-merge-rigid}
  \lean{Graph.exists_partitionDef_le_mergeOn, Graph.deficiencyMerged_le_deficiency,
        Graph.deficiencyMerged_eq_deficiency_of_mem}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:D-deficiency, def:deficiency-merged}
  Let $D \ge 1$ and let $W \subseteq V(G)$ be nonempty with
  $\operatorname{def}(\tilde G[W]) = 0$.
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item For every partition $P$ of $V(G)$, some partition $P'$ coarser
      than $P$, with $W$ inside one of its parts, has
      $\operatorname{def}_{\tilde G}(P) \le \operatorname{def}_{\tilde
      G}(P')$.
    \item For all bodies $a$ and $b$, $\operatorname{def}(\tilde G; a, b)
      \le \operatorname{def}(\tilde G)$, with equality if $a, b \in W$.
  \end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:partitionDef-merge}
  (1) Let $P'$ merge the parts of $P$ that meet $W$ into one part. If $m$
  parts of $P$ meet $W$ and $e$ edges join distinct ones among them,
  merging them lowers the value by $D(m - 1) - (D - 1)e$
  (\cref{lem:partitionDef-merge}). The $e$ edges include the edges of
  $G[W]$ crossing $P|_W$, so this quantity is at most the value of
  $P|_W$ in $G[W]$, which is at most zero.

  (2) The merged deficiency is a maximum over fewer partitions. For the
  equality, merge a partition attaining $\operatorname{def}(\tilde G)$
  along $W$ by (1); the result has $a$ and $b$ in one part.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[A tight set is rigid]
  \label{lem:deficiency-tight-rigid}
  \lean{Graph.deficiency_three_induce_eq_zero_of_tight}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:D-deficiency}
  Let $X \subseteq V(G)$ with $|X| \ge 3$, and suppose $s(Z) \ge 1$ for
  every $Z \subseteq X$ with $|Z| \ge 2$. If $s(X) \le 1$, then
  $\operatorname{def}_3(G[X]) = 0$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:deficiency-singleton-bound}
  Let $P$ be a partition of $X$, with values $v_2(P)$ and $v_3(P)$ in
  $G[X]$. If $P$ has a part $Z$ with $2 \le |Z| < |X|$, then
  $v_2(P) \le s(X) - s(Z) \le 0$
  (\cref{lem:deficiency-singleton-bound}), so $v_3(P) \le 0$. The
  partition $\{X\}$ has $v_3 = 0$. For the partition into singletons,
  $v_2 = s(X) \le 1$ gives $2e(X) \ge 3|X| - 4 \ge 5$, so $e(X) \ge 3$
  and $v_3 = 2s(X) - e(X) < 0$.
\end{proof}

The structural coverage takes either a maximal rigid set, or a set
maximal among the rigid sets that avoid one body $x$ of degree two
(\cref{thm:pencil-x0-theorem-s}). The next lemma treats both, with $X_0$
empty or $X_0 = \{x\}$.

\begin{lemma}[A maximal rigid set bounds the singleton values above it]
  \label{lem:deficiency-core-bound}
  \lean{Graph.deficiency_three_induce_eq_zero_of_le, Graph.partitionDef_induce_id_le_of_maximal}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:D-deficiency}
  Let $G$ be sparse.
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item Let $W \subseteq Y \subseteq V(G)$ with $W$ nonempty and
      $\operatorname{def}_3(G[W]) = 0$. If $s(Y) \le s(W)$, and
      $s(W) \le s(Q)$ for every $Q$ with $W \subseteq Q \subsetneq Y$,
      then $\operatorname{def}_3(G[Y]) = 0$.
    \item Let $W \subseteq V(G)$ be nonempty with
      $\operatorname{def}_3(G[W]) = 0$, and let $X_0$ be empty or a
      single body outside $W$ that sends at most two edges into every
      set avoiding it and at most one into $W$. Suppose $W$ is maximal
      among the sets $W' \supseteq W$ in $V(G) \setminus X_0$ with
      $\operatorname{def}_3(G[W']) = 0$. Then $s(W) \le s(X)$ for every
      $X$ with $W \subseteq X \subseteq V(G)$.
  \end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:deficiency-merge-rigid, lem:deficiency-singleton-bound,
        lem:deficiency-add-body}
  (1) Let $P$ be a partition of $Y$, with values taken in $G[Y]$. Let
  $P'$ be a partition coarser than $P$ with $W$ inside one part $Q$ and
  $v_3(P) \le v_3(P')$ (\cref{lem:deficiency-merge-rigid}). If $Q = Y$
  then $v_3(P') = 0$. Otherwise $v_2(P') \le s(Y) - s(Q) \le 0$
  (\cref{lem:deficiency-singleton-bound}), so $v_3(P') \le 2v_2(P') \le
  0$.

  (2) Suppose not, and take $X \subseteq V(G)$ with $W \subseteq X$ and
  $s(X) < s(W)$, of least size. Then $X \ne W$, and $s(W) \le s(Q)$ for
  every $Q \supseteq W$ with fewer bodies than $X$. If $X$ avoids $X_0$,
  then (1) at $Y = X$ makes $G[X]$ rigid, against the maximality of $W$.
  Otherwise $X_0 = \{x\}$ with $x \in X$; put $Y = X \setminus \{x\}$,
  which contains $W$. Adding $x$ to $Y$ changes the singleton value by
  $3 - 2t$, where $t \le 2$ is the number of edges from $x$ into $Y$
  (\cref{lem:deficiency-add-body}), and $t \le 1$ if $Y = W$. So $Y \ne
  W$, since otherwise $s(X) \ge s(W) + 1$. Then $s(Y) \ge s(W)$, as $Y$
  has fewer bodies than $X$. Also $s(X) \ge s(Y) - 1$, so $s(X) = s(W)
  - 1$ and $s(Y) = s(W)$. Now (1) at $Y$ makes $G[Y]$ rigid, a set
  avoiding $X_0$ that strictly contains $W$, against the maximality of
  $W$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The planar deficiencies of a core and its contraction add up to at most that of the graph]
  \label{lem:deficiency-additive-core}
  \lean{Graph.partitionDef_map, Graph.deficiency_induce_add_deficiency_rigidContract_le}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:D-deficiency, def:rigid-contraction}
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item For a map $c$ of the bodies, the value of a partition of the
      image graph $c(G)$ equals the value of its pull-back along $c$ to
      a partition of $V(G)$.
    \item Let $G$ be sparse, let $r \in W \subseteq V(G)$, and suppose
      $s(W) \le s(X)$ for every $X$ with $W \subseteq X \subseteq V(G)$.
      Let $G/G[W]$ collapse $W$ to $r$ (\cref{def:rigid-contraction}).
      Then
      \[
        \operatorname{def}_2(G[W]) + \operatorname{def}_2(G/G[W]) \;\le\;
        \operatorname{def}_2(G).
      \]
  \end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:deficiency-singleton-bound}
  (1) The parts and the crossing edges correspond under $c$.

  (2) By \cref{lem:deficiency-singleton-bound} and sparsity,
  $\operatorname{def}_2(G[W]) \le s(W)$. Also $s(V(G)) \le
  \operatorname{def}_2(G)$, as $s(V(G))$ is the value of the singletons.
  A partition of $G/G[W]$ pulls back by (1) to a partition of $V(G)$
  with $W$ inside one part $Z$. The deleted edges of $G[W]$ lie inside
  $Z$, so the pull-back has the same value in $G$. That value is at most
  $s(V(G)) - s(Z) \le s(V(G)) - s(W)$
  (\cref{lem:deficiency-singleton-bound}). Adding the three bounds gives
  the claim.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The deficiencies at a body of degree two]
  \label{lem:deficiency-one-body-chain}
  \lean{Graph.not_adj_and_deficiencyMerged_two_add_two_le, Graph.deficiencyMerged_three_add_five_le}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:D-deficiency, def:deficiency-merged}
  Let $V_1 \subseteq V(G)$ and $x \in V(G) \setminus V_1$, and let
  $a \neq b$ in $V_1$ be joined to $x$ by edges.
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item If $G$ is sparse, then $a$ and $b$ are not adjacent, and
      $\operatorname{def}_2(G[V_1]; a, b) + 2 \le
      \operatorname{def}_2(G[V_1])$.
    \item If no set of at least two bodies containing $x$ is rigid, then
      $\operatorname{def}_3(G[V_1]; a, b) + 5 \le
      \operatorname{def}_3(G[V_1])$.
  \end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:deficiency-singleton-bound, lem:deficiency-add-body,
        lem:cut-edge-decomposition}
  (1) An edge $ab$ would give the triangle $\{x, a, b\}$ singleton value
  zero. Let a partition of $V_1$ with $a$ and $b$ in one part $Z$ attain
  the merged deficiency. Its value plus $s(Z)$ is at most
  $s(V_1) \le \operatorname{def}_2(G[V_1])$
  (\cref{lem:deficiency-singleton-bound}), so it suffices that
  $s(Z) \ge 2$. Adding $x$ to $Z$ lowers the singleton value by at least
  one, as $x$ has at least two edges into $Z$
  (\cref{lem:deficiency-add-body}). As $s(Z \cup \{x\}) \ge 1$ by
  sparsity, $s(Z) \ge 2$.

  (2) Suppose otherwise, and let $P$ attain the merged deficiency $m$,
  with $Q$ the part of $a$ and $b$. Refining $Q$ by a partition $R$ of
  $Q$, relabelled apart from the other parts of $P$, changes the value
  of $P$ by the value of $R$ in $G[Q]$
  (\cref{lem:cut-edge-decomposition}). Since
  $\operatorname{def}_3(G[V_1]) \le m + 4$, every such $R$ has value at
  most $4$ in $G[Q]$, and at most $0$ if it keeps $a$ and $b$ together.

  We show that $G[Q \cup \{x\}]$ is rigid, against the hypothesis. Let
  $R'$ be a partition of $Q \cup \{x\}$, and compare its value with that
  of its restriction to $Q$ (\cref{lem:deficiency-add-body}). If $x$ is
  alone, at least its two edges to $a$ and $b$ cross, and the value drops
  by at least $10 - 6 = 4$, to at most $0$. If $x$ shares a part, each
  edge from $x$ to another part costs $5$. When $a$ and $b$ are apart, at
  least one such edge exists, and the value is at most $4 - 5 < 0$. When
  they are together, the value is at most $0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[A non-adjacent pair outside every rigid set]
  \label{lem:deficiency-two-body-chain}
  \lean{Graph.deficiencyMerged_two_add_two_le}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:D-deficiency, def:deficiency-merged}
  Let $G$ be sparse, and let $a \neq b$ be bodies of $G$ that are not
  adjacent, with $\operatorname{def}_3(G; a, b) \neq
  \operatorname{def}_3(G)$. Then $\operatorname{def}_2(G; a, b) + 2 \le
  \operatorname{def}_2(G)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{lem:deficiency-singleton-bound, lem:deficiency-add-body,
        lem:deficiency-tight-rigid, lem:deficiency-merge-rigid}
  Otherwise the part $Z$ of $a$ and $b$ in a partition attaining the
  planar merged deficiency has $s(Z) \le 1$
  (\cref{lem:deficiency-singleton-bound}). The pair $\{a, b\}$ has
  singleton value $3$, as $a$ and $b$ are not adjacent
  (\cref{lem:deficiency-add-body}), so $|Z| \ge 3$, and $Z$ is rigid
  (\cref{lem:deficiency-tight-rigid}). Then $\operatorname{def}_3(G; a,
  b) = \operatorname{def}_3(G)$ (\cref{lem:deficiency-merge-rigid}).
\end{proof}

\subsection{The structural coverage}
\label{sec:main-component-coverage}

This subsection completes the induction on the number of bodies. Call a
graph \emph{smaller than} $G$ if it satisfies the standing hypotheses
(\cref{def:pencil-x0-standing}) and has fewer bodies than $G$. Each step
of the preceding subsections applies to a graph $G$ that satisfies the
standing hypotheses and some further conditions. It assumes that the
general configurations of none, one or two graphs with fewer bodies
attain. We say that $G$ \emph{reduces} to a property $\mathcal P$ of
graphs if $G$ meets the further conditions of some step, and the graphs
that this step assumes to attain satisfy $\mathcal P$
(\cref{def:pencil-x0-reduces}). If every graph satisfying the standing
hypotheses reduces to the graphs smaller than it, then the general
configuration of every such graph attains, by strong induction on the
number of bodies (\cref{thm:pencil-x0-reduction-attains}). The
structural coverage proves that every such graph does reduce, by a case
analysis (\cref{thm:pencil-x0-coverage}).

The table below lists the cases. The proof tests the first five in
order, so each of them assumes that those above it fail. Once all five
fail, $G$ is 2-connected, sparse (\cref{sec:main-component-sparse}) and
not a cycle. Its bodies of degree two then lie on \emph{chains}: paths
of bodies of degree two between bodies of degree at least three
(\cref{def:pencil-x0-chain}). We write a chain as an open ear
$a - x_1 - \cdots - x_k - b$ on $V_1$ (\cref{sec:main-component-chain}).
A chain is \emph{usable} if it meets the condition of one of the chain
rows of the table (\cref{def:pencil-x0-usable-chain}), and then the step
of that row applies (\cref{lem:pencil-x0-chain-reduces}). If no chain is
usable, the last row applies. For each case the table names the step
that settles it, and the smaller graphs whose attainment that step
assumes. In the first two rows $V_1$ and $V_2$ are the two sides of a
cut vertex or of a chain of bridges (\cref{sec:main-component-cut}), and
$W$ is the set contracted.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{p{5.4cm}p{4.4cm}p{2.8cm}}
  \raggedright \emph{Case}
    & \raggedright \emph{Step}
    & \raggedright \emph{Smaller graphs} \tabularnewline
  \hline
  \raggedright $G$ is not $2$-edge-connected
    & \raggedright a chain of bridges (\cref{thm:pencil-x0-bridge})
    & \raggedright $G[V_1]$, $G[V_2]$ \tabularnewline
  \raggedright $G - v$ is disconnected for some body $v$
    & \raggedright a cut vertex (\cref{thm:pencil-x0-cut})
    & \raggedright $G[V_1]$, $G[V_2]$ \tabularnewline
  \raggedright every body has degree two
    & \raggedright a cycle (\cref{thm:pencil-x0-cycle})
    & \raggedright none \tabularnewline
  \raggedright $\operatorname{def}_2(G) = \operatorname{def}_3(G)$
    & \raggedright equal deficiencies (\cref{cor:pencil-jj-flat})
    & \raggedright none \tabularnewline
  \raggedright a planar-rigid set
    & \raggedright contraction at a maximal planar-rigid set $W$
      (\cref{thm:pencil-x0-contract-rigid})
    & \raggedright $G/G[W]$ \tabularnewline
  \raggedright a usable chain with $k = 3$
    & \raggedright an open ear (\cref{thm:pencil-x0-open-ear-three})
    & \raggedright $G[V_1]$, and $G$ with $x_2$ suppressed \tabularnewline
  \raggedright a usable chain with $k = 4$
    & \raggedright an open ear (\cref{thm:pencil-x0-open-ear-four})
    & \raggedright $G[V_1]$, and $G$ with $x_2$ suppressed \tabularnewline
  \raggedright a usable chain with $k \ge 5$
    & \raggedright an open ear (\cref{thm:pencil-x0-open-ear})
    & \raggedright $G[V_1]$ \tabularnewline
  \raggedright a usable chain with $k = 2$ and
    $\operatorname{def}_3(G[V_1]; a, b) = \operatorname{def}_3(G[V_1])$
    & \raggedright an open ear (\cref{thm:pencil-x0-open-ear-two})
    & \raggedright $G[V_1]$ \tabularnewline
  \raggedright a usable chain with $k = 2$, $a$ and $b$ not adjacent,
    and $\operatorname{def}_2(G[V_1]; a, b) + 2 \le
    \operatorname{def}_2(G[V_1])$
    & \raggedright an open ear (\cref{thm:pencil-x0-open-ear-two-orbit})
    & \raggedright $G[V_1]$, and $G$ with $x_2$ suppressed \tabularnewline
  \raggedright a usable chain with $k = 1$ and
    $\operatorname{def}_3(G[V_1]; a, b) = \operatorname{def}_3(G[V_1])$
    & \raggedright an open ear (\cref{thm:pencil-x0-open-ear-one})
    & \raggedright $G[V_1]$ \tabularnewline
  \raggedright a usable chain with $k = 1$ and
    $\operatorname{def}_3(G[V_1]; a, b) + 5 \le
    \operatorname{def}_3(G[V_1])$
    & \raggedright splitting off $x_1$ (\cref{thm:pencil-x0-splitoff})
    & \raggedright $G$ with $x_1$ suppressed \tabularnewline
  \raggedright no usable chain
    & \raggedright contraction at an additive core $W$
      (\cref{thm:pencil-x0-contract-additive})
    & \raggedright $G[W]$, $G/G[W]$ \tabularnewline
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The chain rows are the conditions of \cref{def:pencil-x0-usable-chain},
with $k \ge 3$ split by the step. A chain may meet two of them, and then
either step applies. \Cref{rem:pencil-x0-theta} follows these cases
through the $\theta$-graphs.

In the last row, \cref{thm:pencil-x0-theorem-s} finds the core. Its
proof has three steps. First, every chain has one or two interior
bodies, as a chain with more is usable. Second, each chain gives a rigid
set. With two interior bodies its ends are adjacent, and the chain and
its ends form a $4$-cycle, which is rigid. With one interior body, that
body lies in a rigid set. Third, a maximal rigid set containing the set
found is the core. In a sparse graph a maximal rigid set has the least
singleton value among the sets containing it. So the planar deficiencies
of the core and of the contraction add up to at most that of $G$
(\cref{sec:main-component-sparse}). When $G$ itself is rigid, a maximal
rigid set is all of $V(G)$, and we take the core maximal among the rigid
sets that avoid one body of degree two.

\begin{definition}[One step of the induction]
  \label{def:pencil-x0-reduces}
  \lean{Graph.IsOpenEar, Graph.X0Reduces}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-x0-standing, def:D-deficiency, def:deficiency-merged,
        def:graph-operations, def:rigid-contraction, def:cut-edges-2ec}
  Let $\mathcal P$ be a property of graphs, and let open ears be as in
  \cref{sec:main-component-chain}. The graph $G$ \emph{reduces to
  $\mathcal P$} if one of the following holds.
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item $\operatorname{def}_2(G) = \operatorname{def}_3(G)$.
    \item $G$ has a cut vertex with sides $V_1$ and $V_2$ as in
      \cref{thm:pencil-x0-cut}, and $G[V_1]$ and $G[V_2]$ satisfy
      $\mathcal P$.
    \item $G$ has a chain of bridges between $V_1$ and $V_2$ as in
      \cref{thm:pencil-x0-bridge}, and $G[V_1]$ and $G[V_2]$ satisfy
      $\mathcal P$.
    \item $G$ is a cycle, given as an edge $ab$ and a path from $a$ to
      $b$ through its other bodies, as in \cref{thm:pencil-x0-cycle}.
    \item $G$ has an open ear $a - x_1 - \cdots - x_k - b$ on $V_1$,
      $G[V_1]$ satisfies $\mathcal P$, and one of the following holds:
      $k \ge 5$; $k \in \{3, 4\}$ and $G$ with $x_2$ suppressed
      satisfies $\mathcal P$; $k = 2$ and $\operatorname{def}_3(G[V_1])
      \le \operatorname{def}_3(G)$; $k = 2$, $a$ and $b$ are not
      adjacent, $\operatorname{def}_2(G[V_1]; a, b) + 2 \le
      \operatorname{def}_2(G[V_1])$ and $G$ with $x_2$ suppressed
      satisfies $\mathcal P$; or $k = 1$, $a$ and $b$ are not adjacent,
      $\operatorname{def}_3(G[V_1]) \le \operatorname{def}_3(G)$ and
      $\operatorname{def}_2(G[V_1]; a, b) + 2 \le
      \operatorname{def}_2(G[V_1])$.
    \item $G$ has an open ear $a - x - b$ on $V_1$ whose ends are not
      adjacent, $\operatorname{def}_3(G[V_1]; a, b) + 5 \le
      \operatorname{def}_3(G[V_1])$, and $G$ with $x$ suppressed
      satisfies $\mathcal P$.
    \item $G$ is 2-edge-connected, and there are $W \subsetneq V(G)$
      with $|W| \ge 2$, no body outside $W$ adjacent to two bodies of
      $W$, and $r \in W$, such that $G/G[W]$ (collapsing $W$ to $r$)
      satisfies $\mathcal P$ and either $\operatorname{def}_2(G[W]) =
      0$, or $\operatorname{def}_3(G[W]) = 0$,
      $\operatorname{def}_2(G[W]) + \operatorname{def}_2(G/G[W]) \le
      \operatorname{def}_2(G)$ and $G[W]$ satisfies $\mathcal P$.
  \end{enumerate}
  Here suppressing an interior body $x_i$ is the splitting-off
  $G_{x_i}^{x_{i-1} x_{i+1}}$ (\cref{def:graph-operations}, with $x_0 =
  a$ and $x_{k+1} = b$) whose new edge carries the label of the edge
  $x_{i-1} x_i$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[The induction on the number of bodies]
  \label{thm:pencil-x0-reduction-attains}
  \lean{Graph.X0Reduces.x0Attains, Graph.X0Attains.of_isX0Graph_of_x0Reduces}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-x0-reduces, def:pencil-x0-attains,
        def:pencil-x0-standing}
  Let $K$ be infinite.
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item Let $G$ satisfy the standing hypotheses and reduce to
      $\mathcal P$. If the general configuration of every graph
      satisfying $\mathcal P$ attains, then the general configuration of
      $G$ attains.
    \item If every graph satisfying the standing hypotheses reduces to
      the graphs smaller than it, then the general configuration of
      every graph satisfying the standing hypotheses attains.
  \end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  \uses{cor:pencil-jj-flat, thm:pencil-x0-cut, thm:pencil-x0-bridge,
        thm:pencil-x0-cycle, thm:pencil-x0-open-ear,
        thm:pencil-x0-open-ear-four, thm:pencil-x0-open-ear-three,
        thm:pencil-x0-open-ear-two, thm:pencil-x0-open-ear-two-orbit,
        thm:pencil-x0-open-ear-one, thm:pencil-x0-splitoff,
        thm:pencil-x0-contract-rigid, thm:pencil-x0-contract-additive}
  (1) Each clause of \cref{def:pencil-x0-reduces}, and each alternative
  in clauses (5) and (7), states the further conditions of one step, with
  $\mathcal P$ at the graphs that the step assumes to attain. The steps
  are equal deficiencies (\cref{cor:pencil-jj-flat}), the cut vertex
  and the chain of bridges (\cref{thm:pencil-x0-cut},
  \cref{thm:pencil-x0-bridge}), the cycle (\cref{thm:pencil-x0-cycle}),
  the open ears (\cref{thm:pencil-x0-open-ear},
  \cref{thm:pencil-x0-open-ear-four},
  \cref{thm:pencil-x0-open-ear-three},
  \cref{thm:pencil-x0-open-ear-two},
  \cref{thm:pencil-x0-open-ear-two-orbit},
  \cref{thm:pencil-x0-open-ear-one}), the split-off
  (\cref{thm:pencil-x0-splitoff}) and the two contractions
  (\cref{thm:pencil-x0-contract-rigid},
  \cref{thm:pencil-x0-contract-additive}). As every graph satisfying
  $\mathcal P$ attains, the step applies.

  (2) Strong induction on the number of bodies, by (1) with $\mathcal P$
  the property of being smaller than $G$.
\end{proof}

The rest of the subsection shows that $G$ reduces in each case of the
table. We begin with the chains, and with the standing hypotheses at the
smaller graphs that a chain gives.

\begin{definition}[Chains]
  \label{def:pencil-x0-chain}
  \lean{Graph.IsChain}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-x0-reduces}
  A \emph{chain} of $G$ is an open ear $a - x_1 - \cdots - x_k - b$ on
  $V_1$ with $k \ge 1$ whose interior bodies all have degree two in $G$
  and whose ends have degree at least three.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Every body of degree two lies on a chain]
  \label{lem:pencil-x0-chain-exists}
  \lean{Graph.IsX0Graph.exists_isChain}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-x0-chain, def:pencil-x0-standing}
  Let $G$ satisfy the standing hypotheses, with $G - v$ connected for
  every body $v$ and some body of degree other than two. Then every body
  of degree two is an interior body of a chain.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  Extend a path through the body along bodies of degree two as far as
  possible (\texttt{Graph.IsOpenEar.exists\_maximal}). An interior body
  has both of its neighbours on the path. So if an end had degree two,
  its other neighbour would either extend the path or be the other end.
  In the second case the path and that edge form a cycle. Either the
  cycle is all of $G$, and every body of $G$ has degree two, or the cycle
  meets the rest of $G$ only at the other end, which is then a cut
  vertex. So both ends have degree at least three.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The standing hypotheses at the smaller graphs of a chain]
  \label{lem:pencil-x0-chain-standing}
  \lean{Graph.IsChain.isX0Graph_induce, Graph.IsX0Graph.splitOff}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:pencil-x0-chain, def:pencil-x0-standing,
        def:graph-operations}
  Let $G$ satisfy the standing hypotheses.
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item If $G - v$ is connected for every body $v$, then $G[V_1]$
      satisfies the standing hypotheses for every chain on $V_1$.
    \item Let a body $v$ have exactly two edges, to distinct bodies $u$
      and $w$ that are not adjacent. Then the splitting-off $G_v^{uw}$,
      whose new edge carries the label of the edge $vu$, satisfies the
      standing hypotheses.
  \end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \leanok
  (1) The ends of the chain keep at least two neighbours in $V_1$, and
  every other body of $V_1$ keeps all of them. In $G - x_1$ no body of
  $V_1$ other than $b$ has a neighbour outside $V_1$. So if $G[V_1]$ were
  disconnected, a part of it that avoids $b$ would be left by no edge of
  $G - x_1$, which is connected
  (\texttt{Graph.Connected.induce\_of\_gate}).

  (2) The new edge is not parallel to an old one, since $u$ and $w$ are
  not adjacent, and $u$ and $w$ each exchange the edge to $v$ for the
  new edge. A set of bodies left by no edge of $G_v^{uw}$ contains both
  or neither of $u$ and $w$, and then, with $v$ added in the first case,
  it is left by no edge of $G$.
